                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e522] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: table/tab1/
                - generic [ref=e523]:
                  - heading "2.1. Modified single amino acid cofactors" [level=3] [ref=e524]
                  - paragraph [ref=e525]: This subsection focuses on single amino acid residues that undergo PTMs to form non-radical cofactors. Several examples illustrate the diverse chemical transformations involved.
                  - generic [ref=e526]:
                    - heading "2.1.1. Carboxylated lysine" [level=4] [ref=e527]
                    - paragraph [ref=e528]:
                      - text: During the characterization of the beticolin 1 biosynthetic pathway, a novel non-heme iron-dependent oxygenase, BTG13, was recently identified as the catalyst for anthraquinone ring cleavage.
                      - superscript [ref=e529]:
                        - link "45" [ref=e530] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit45"
                      - text: Structural analysis of wild-type BTG13 (
                      - link "Fig. 3A" [ref=e531] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig3"
                      - text: ) reveals an unprecedented iron coordination environment comprising four histidines and a carboxylated lysine (Kcx) residue at position 377 (
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e532] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig1"
                      - text: ", 1), which is hydrogen-bonded to His58 and Thr299."
                      - superscript [ref=e533]:
                        - link "45,63" [ref=e534] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit45"
                      - text: This Kcx377 acts as a crucial protein-derived cofactor in the oxygenase. Substitution of Lys377 with tyrosine or arginine leads to a complete loss of catalytic activity in BTG13, demonstrating the essential role of Kcx377 in catalysis. Further structural characterization of the H58F and T299V variants reveals a perturbed Kcx377 conformation in H58F and the absence of Kcx377 in T299V, strongly implicating His58 and Thr299 in Kcx377 biogenesis.
                      - superscript [ref=e535]:
                        - link "45" [ref=e536] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit45"
                      - text: While the precise mechanism of Kcx377 formation remains to be fully elucidated, these findings underscore the critical roles of His58 and Thr299 in regulating the process of Lys377 carboxylation and, consequently, enzyme reactivity.
                    - figure [ref=e537]:
                      - heading "Fig. 3. Crystal structures showing novel lysine PTM's. (A) Iron-binding motif in BTG13 (yellow; PDB 7Y3W) is comprised of four histidine residues, a carboxylated lysine residue, and a water molecule. Thr299 and His58 from the second coordination sphere are shown due to their implications in regulation of Lys377 carboxylation (H-bonding shown in gray dashed lines). (B) Active site of LarA (orange; PDB 4YNS) showing the (SCS)Ni(ii) pincer complex with Lys184 and His200 potentially playing roles in catalysis/Ni-binding (coordination shown in gray dashed lines)." [level=5] [ref=e538]
                      - paragraph [ref=e539]:
                        - link "Fig. 3 🔍" [ref=e540] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f3.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 3" [ref=e541]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e543] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/fig3/
                    - paragraph [ref=e544]:
                      - text: Mechanistic investigations into the C
                      - subscript [ref=e545]: 4a
                      - text: –C
                      - subscript [ref=e546]: "10"
                      - text: bond cleavage of anthraquinone catalyzed by BTG13, supported by computational studies, propose a stepwise mechanism initiated by hydrogen atom abstraction from C
                      - subscript [ref=e547]: "10"
                      - text: of the substrate by a ferric superoxide species, Fe(iii)–O
                      - subscript [ref=e548]: "2"
                      - text: ˙
                      - superscript [ref=e549]: −
                      - text: ", generating a substrate radical–Fe(iii)–OOH intermediate."
                      - superscript [ref=e550]:
                        - link "63" [ref=e551] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit63"
                      - text: Subsequent homolytic O–O bond cleavage and rebound of the distal oxygen to the substrate radical forms a high-valent Fe(iv)
                      - generic "DOUBLE BOND, LENGTH AS M-DASH" [ref=e552]:
                        - img [ref=e553]
                      - text: O species. Computational studies further suggest that Kcx377 facilitates the initial electron transfer from the iron center to dioxygen, promoting the formation of Fe(iii)–O
                      - subscript [ref=e556]: "2"
                      - text: ˙
                      - superscript [ref=e557]: −
                      - text: ", the catalytically relevant species."
                      - superscript [ref=e558]:
                        - link "63" [ref=e559] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit63"
                  - generic [ref=e560]:
                    - heading "2.1.2. Lysine acetylation regulates enzyme activity" [level=4] [ref=e561]
                    - paragraph [ref=e562]:
                      - text: Lysine acetylation (
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e563] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig1"
                      - text: ", 2) is widespread across all domains of life."
                      - superscript [ref=e564]:
                        - link "64,65" [ref=e565] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit64"
                      - text: Although it is generally understood that lysine acetylation regulates AMP-forming acetyl-CoA synthetase (AcsA) in bacteria and eukaryotes, the mechanism of action is yet to be elucidated. In AcsA of
                      - emphasis [ref=e566]: Bacillus subtilis
                      - text: ", the acetylation of Lys549 regulates its activity."
                      - superscript [ref=e567]:
                        - link "66" [ref=e568] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit66"
                      - text: This lysine residue is conserved in AcsA in bacteria and eukaryotes, suggesting that regulation of enzyme activity through lysine acetylation is evolutionarily conserved.
                      - superscript [ref=e569]:
                        - link "49" [ref=e570] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit49"
                    - paragraph [ref=e571]:
                      - text: A recently characterized protein, AcuA, functions as an acetyltransferase, transferring an acetyl group from either acetyl-CoA or acetyl phosphate to Lys549 on AcsA, thereby inactivating this synthetase.
                      - superscript [ref=e572]:
                        - link "49" [ref=e573] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit49"
                      - text: Interestingly, this process is reversible, and AcsA activity can be restored through the enzymatic deacetylation of Lys549.
                      - superscript [ref=e574]:
                        - link "49" [ref=e575] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit49"
                      - text: The presence of genetically encoded regulatory mechanisms—namely, an acetyltransferase and its corresponding deacetylase—highlights the critical need for AcsA regulation. Given the conservation of this lysine residue, it is speculated that other acetyl-CoA synthetases might regulate their activity similarly, based on metabolic conditions. These findings enhance our understanding of the AMP-forming acetyl-CoA synthetase in cellular metabolism and present potential therapeutic targets for interventions.
                  - generic [ref=e576]:
                    - heading "2.1.3. First pincer complex identified in nature" [level=4] [ref=e577]
                    - paragraph [ref=e578]:
                      - text: Pincer ligands are well-established in synthetic organometallic chemistry, prized for their robust coordination properties and ability to stabilize a wide range of metal oxidation states. However, before the discovery of the pyridinium-3-thioamide-5-thiocarboxylic acid mononucleotide Ni pincer complex, (SCS)Ni(ii), in the lactate racemase of
                      - emphasis [ref=e579]: Lactobacillus plantarum
                      - text: (LarA),
                      - superscript [ref=e580]:
                        - link "51" [ref=e581] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit51"
                      - text: such ligands were unknown in biological systems (
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e582] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig1"
                      - text: ", 3). LarA is a nickel-dependent enzyme requiring three auxiliary proteins, LarB, LarC, and LarE, for activation ("
                      - link "Fig. 4" [ref=e583] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig4"
                      - text: ).
                      - superscript [ref=e584]:
                        - link "67,68" [ref=e585] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit67"
                      - text: While the precise roles of these proteins and the mechanism of nickel incorporation were initially unclear, recent studies have elucidated this complex process. Site-directed mutagenesis, coupled with structural analysis, implicated Lys184 in nickel coordination or catalysis, revealing a novel posttranslational modification involving the attachment of a nicotinic acid mononucleotide (NAMN) derivative to its carboxyl group.
                      - superscript [ref=e586]:
                        - link "51" [ref=e587] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit51"
                    - figure [ref=e588]:
                      - heading "Fig. 4. Proposed biogenesis mechanism of (SCS)Ni(ii) pincer complex. LarB is responsible for the formation of P2CMN from NaAD in a CO2/bicarbonate dependent reaction. Two LarE molecules are required for P2TMN formation. LarE activates P2CMN via adenylylation, covalently links the protein to P2CMN, and sacrifices a sulfur atom from its own cysteine residue to form the thioacid of P2CMN, a second LarE protomer repeats the set of reactions, where Dha is released as a product upon sulfur atom donation, forming P2TMN. LarC is suggested to be responsible for Ni insertion into P2TMN and transferring the cofactor to LarA (the straight arrow in the pincer indicates coordination)." [level=5] [ref=e589]:
                        - text: Fig. 4. Proposed biogenesis mechanism of (SCS)Ni(ii) pincer complex. LarB is responsible for the formation of P2CMN from NaAD in a CO
                        - subscript [ref=e590]: "2"
                        - text: /bicarbonate dependent reaction. Two LarE molecules are required for P2TMN formation. LarE activates P2CMN
                        - emphasis [ref=e591]: via
                        - text: adenylylation, covalently links the protein to P2CMN, and sacrifices a sulfur atom from its own cysteine residue to form the thioacid of P2CMN, a second LarE protomer repeats the set of reactions, where Dha is released as a product upon sulfur atom donation, forming P2TMN. LarC is suggested to be responsible for Ni insertion into P2TMN and transferring the cofactor to LarA (the straight arrow in the pincer indicates coordination).
                      - paragraph [ref=e592]:
                        - link "Fig. 4 🔍" [ref=e593] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f4.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 4" [ref=e594]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e596] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/fig4/
                    - paragraph [ref=e597]:
                      - text: A comprehensive investigation of the cofactor biosynthetic pathway jointly by Hausinger and Hu has defined the functions of the three auxiliary proteins starting from a NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e598]: +
                      - text: precursor, nicotinic acid adenine dinucleotide (NaAD).
                      - superscript [ref=e599]:
                        - link "52" [ref=e600] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit52"
                      - text: LarB initiates cofactor assembly by carboxylating and hydrolyzing the pyridine ring of NaAD to yield AMP and pyridinium-3,5-biscarboxylic acid mononucleotide (P2CMN).
                      - superscript [ref=e601]:
                        - link "69,70" [ref=e602] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit69"
                      - text: Subsequent reactions with two LarE molecules, involving sacrificial desulfurization of Cys176, convert P2CMN to the thiocarboxylated intermediate, P2TMN.
                      - superscript [ref=e603]:
                        - link "71–74" [ref=e604] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit71"
                      - text: LarC purifies with bound nickel,
                      - superscript [ref=e605]:
                        - link "52" [ref=e606] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit52"
                      - text: and it is a CTP-dependent nickel-inserting cyclometallase playing the role in nickel insertion into P2TMN and subsequent transfer of the completed (SCS)Ni(ii) cofactor to LarA (
                      - link "Fig. 3B" [ref=e607] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig3"
                      - text: ).
                      - superscript [ref=e608]:
                        - link "75" [ref=e609] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit75"
                    - paragraph [ref=e610]:
                      - text: The identification of this unprecedented enzyme-generated pincer complex essential for catalyzing the racemization of lactate raises the possibility of similar cofactors in other biological systems. The broad distribution of LarB, LarC, and LarE homologs beyond organisms possessing LarA strongly suggests the potential for additional pincer-dependent enzymes.
                      - superscript [ref=e611]:
                        - link "51,76" [ref=e612] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit51"
                  - generic [ref=e613]:
                    - heading "2.1.4. Various cofactors in Methyl CoM reductases" [level=4] [ref=e614]
                    - paragraph [ref=e615]:
                      - text: Methyl-coenzyme M reductase (MCR), one of the ten nickel-dependent enzymes
                      - superscript [ref=e616]:
                        - link "77" [ref=e617] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit77"
                      - text: catalyzing methane formation in methanogenic archaea,
                      - superscript [ref=e618]:
                        - link "78" [ref=e619] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit78"
                      - text: is a rich source of posttranslationally modified amino acids (
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e620] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig1"
                      - text: ", 4–14)."
                      - superscript [ref=e621]:
                        - link "79" [ref=e622] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit79"
                      - text: Structural studies of MCR in
                      - emphasis [ref=e623]: Methanobacterium thermoautotrophicum
                      - text: "have revealed a cluster of five modified residues near the active site:"
                      - emphasis [ref=e624]: S
                      - text: "-methyl-cysteine, 5-C-("
                      - emphasis [ref=e625]: S
                      - text: )-methyl-arginine, 1-
                      - emphasis [ref=e626]: "N"
                      - text: "-methylhistidine, 2-C-("
                      - emphasis [ref=e627]: S
                      - text: )-methyl-glutamine, and thio-glycine.
                      - superscript [ref=e628]:
                        - link "25" [ref=e629] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit25"
                      - text: While 5-C-(
                      - emphasis [ref=e630]: S
                      - text: )-methyl-arginine, 1-
                      - emphasis [ref=e631]: "N"
                      - text: "-methyl-histidine, and thio-glycine are universally conserved across methanogens, the presence of"
                      - emphasis [ref=e632]: S
                      - text: "-methyl-cysteine and 2-C-("
                      - emphasis [ref=e633]: S
                      - text: )-methyl-glutamine varies among species.
                      - superscript [ref=e634]:
                        - link "42" [ref=e635] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit42"
                    - paragraph [ref=e636]:
                      - text: Further investigations of MCR homologs, including ethyl CoM reductase (ECR), MCR from
                      - emphasis [ref=e637]: Methanothermobacter marburgensis
                      - text: ", and MCR from anaerobic methanotrophic archaea (ANME-1), have unveiled a diverse repertoire of novel PTMs. These include 6-hydroxy-tryptophan, 7-hydroxy-tryptophan, didehydro-aspartate,"
                      - emphasis [ref=e638]: S
                      - text: "-oxy-methionine, 3-methyl-isoleucine, and 2-"
                      - emphasis [ref=e639]: "N"
                      - text: "-methyl-histidine."
                      - superscript [ref=e640]:
                        - link "42,43" [ref=e641] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit42"
                      - text: Notably, didehydro-aspartate, was discovered in MCRs from
                      - emphasis [ref=e642]: M. marburgensis
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e643]: Methanosarcina barkeri
                      - text: through peptide-sequencing and mass spectrometry and confirmed by a 2.15 Å resolution crystal structure.
                      - superscript [ref=e644]:
                        - link "27" [ref=e645] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit27"
                      - text: The formation of the double bond in didehydro-aspartate restricts the conformational space of the side chain, reducing the mobility of the carboxylate group and decreasing the residue's p
                      - emphasis [ref=e646]: K
                      - subscript [ref=e647]: a
                      - text: . It is proposed that this modification fine-tunes enzyme structure to optimize substrate-binding and catalysis.
                      - superscript [ref=e648]:
                        - link "27" [ref=e649] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit27"
                    - paragraph [ref=e650]:
                      - text: The distribution of these modifications varies across different MCRs. For example, ANME-1 harbors 7-hydroxy-tryptophan and
                      - emphasis [ref=e651]: S
                      - text: "-oxymethionine,"
                      - superscript [ref=e652]:
                        - link "80" [ref=e653] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit80"
                      - text: while MCR from
                      - emphasis [ref=e654]: Methanotorris formicicus
                      - text: contains 6-hydroxy-tryptophan.
                      - superscript [ref=e655]:
                        - link "81" [ref=e656] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit81"
                      - text: 3-Methyl-isoleucine and 2-
                      - emphasis [ref=e657]: "N"
                      - text: "-methyl-histidine have been identified in the structure of"
                      - emphasis [ref=e658]: Candidatus
                      - text: Ethanoperedens thermophilum MCR.
                      - superscript [ref=e659]:
                        - link "82" [ref=e660] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit82"
                      - text: The non-overlapping occurrence of 7-hydroxy-tryptophan and methylated arginine in ANME-1 suggests potential compensatory functions for these modifications.
                      - superscript [ref=e661]:
                        - link "80" [ref=e662] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit80"
                      - text: Similarly, 6-hydroxy-tryptophan may compensate for the absence of didehydro-aspartate in MCR.
                      - superscript [ref=e663]:
                        - link "81" [ref=e664] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit81"
                      - text: However, the precise roles and biosynthetic origins of many of these PTMs remain to be fully elucidated.
                    - paragraph [ref=e665]:
                      - text: Significant progress has been made in understanding the biosynthesis of
                      - emphasis [ref=e666]: S
                      - text: "-methyl-cysteine, 2-C-("
                      - emphasis [ref=e667]: S
                      - text: )-methyl-glutamine, 5-C-(
                      - emphasis [ref=e668]: S
                      - text: )-methyl-arginine, and thio-glycine.
                      - superscript [ref=e669]:
                        - link "83" [ref=e670] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit83"
                      - text: Gene knockout studies on the α subunit of MCR (McrA) have shown that the
                      - emphasis [ref=e671]: ycaO-tfuA
                      - text: locus is required for thio-glycine formation, where phylogenetic analyses showed their prevalence in methanogens.
                      - superscript [ref=e672]:
                        - link "37" [ref=e673] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit37"
                      - text: Thio-glycine, present in all examined MCRs, is implicated in stabilizing secondary protein structure near the active site, although its formation mechanism is still under investigation.
                      - superscript [ref=e674]:
                        - link "37" [ref=e675] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit37"
                      - text: A SAM-dependent methyltransferase, encoded by
                      - emphasis [ref=e676]: mcmA
                      - text: ", is hypothesized to be responsible for the methyl-cysteine modification."
                      - superscript [ref=e677]:
                        - link "38" [ref=e678] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit38"
                      - text: Studies on MCR mutants lacking thio-glycine and methyl-cysteine showed significant growth defects, suggesting a synergistic interaction between these two modifications.
                      - superscript [ref=e679]:
                        - link "38" [ref=e680] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit38"
                      - text: While histidine methylation is conserved in all methanogens, the specific enzyme remains unidentified, although a SAM-dependent methyltransferase is suspected. It is hypothesized that this methylation positions the imidazole that coordinates coenzyme B, CoB.
                      - superscript [ref=e681]:
                        - link "84" [ref=e682] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit84"
                    - paragraph [ref=e683]:
                      - text: The methylation of arginine in MCR is catalyzed by the radical SAM methyltransferase MA4551 (Mmp10), also known as arginine C-methyltransferase (RCMT).
                      - superscript [ref=e684]:
                        - link "26" [ref=e685] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit26"
                      - text: RCMT utilizes methylcobalamin (MeCbl) as a cofactor.
                      - superscript [ref=e686]:
                        - link "26,85" [ref=e687] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit26"
                      - text: Booker
                      - emphasis [ref=e688]: et al.
                      - text: classified
                      - emphasis [ref=e689]: Ma
                      - text: Mmp10 (Mmp10 from
                      - emphasis [ref=e690]: Methanosarcina acetivorans
                      - text: ) as a new member of subclass B in the radical SAM methyltransferase family, revealing a C-terminal cobalamin-binding domain.
                      - superscript [ref=e691]:
                        - link "86" [ref=e692] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit86"
                      - text: EPR studies have shown that a tyrosine residue coordinates the [4Fe–4S] cluster, enabling both radical and nucleophilic chemistry,
                      - superscript [ref=e693]:
                        - link "85" [ref=e694] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit85"
                      - text: similar to the initial steps of tyrosine cleavage by HydG of the radical SAM superfamily
                      - superscript [ref=e695]:
                        - link "87,88" [ref=e696] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit87"
                      - text: for cyanide production.
                      - superscript [ref=e697]:
                        - link "89,90" [ref=e698] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit89"
                      - text: The proposed mechanism involves SAM binding, assisted by tyrosine displacement in the [4Fe–4S] cluster. The 5′-deoxyadenosyl radical abstracts a hydrogen atom from arginine, initiating methyl transfer from MeCbl.
                      - superscript [ref=e699]:
                        - link "85" [ref=e700] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit85"
                      - text: The resulting Co(ii) intermediate is likely reduced back to MeCbl by a ferredoxin (
                      - link "Fig. 5" [ref=e701] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig5"
                      - text: ). Similarly, glutamine methylation in MCR is catalyzed by an enzyme of class B radical SAM methyltransferases, glutamine C-methyltransferase (QCMT), which utilizes a [4Fe–4S] cluster and MeCbl similarly. Studies have shown that QCMT transfers a methyl group from SAM to cobalamin(i), regenerating MeCbl, with 5′-deoxyadenosine and
                      - emphasis [ref=e702]: S
                      - text: "-adenosyl-l-homocysteine as co-products."
                      - superscript [ref=e703]:
                        - link "38,91" [ref=e704] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit38"
                      - text: The similarities between QCMT and RCMT suggest analogous catalytic mechanisms.
                    - figure [ref=e705]:
                      - heading "Fig. 5. Proposed catalytic cycle of arginine C-methyltransferase (RCMT), also known as Mmp10, to produce 5-C-(S)-methylarginine, methionine, 5′-deoxyadenosine, and S-adenosyl-l-homocysteine.85." [level=5] [ref=e706]:
                        - text: Fig. 5. Proposed catalytic cycle of arginine C-methyltransferase (RCMT), also known as Mmp10, to produce 5-C-(
                        - emphasis [ref=e707]: S
                        - text: )-methylarginine, methionine, 5′-deoxyadenosine, and
                        - emphasis [ref=e708]: S
                        - text: "-adenosyl-l-homocysteine."
                        - superscript [ref=e709]:
                          - link "85" [ref=e710] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#cit85"
                        - text: .
                      - paragraph [ref=e711]:
                        - link "Fig. 5 🔍" [ref=e712] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f5.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 5" [ref=e713]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e715] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/fig5/
                    - paragraph [ref=e716]:
                      - text: Intriguingly, the methylation of glutamine in MCR is also catalyzed by a class B radical SAM methyltransferase, termed glutamine C-methyltransferase (QCMT).
                      - superscript [ref=e717]:
                        - link "39" [ref=e718] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit39"
                      - text: Using the putative QCMT from
                      - emphasis [ref=e719]: Methanoculleus thermophilus
                      - text: ", Layer"
                      - emphasis [ref=e720]: et al.
                      - text: demonstrated glutamine methylation in an MCR-derived peptide substrate.
                      - superscript [ref=e721]:
                        - link "39" [ref=e722] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit39"
                      - text: QCMT possesses a single [4Fe–4S] cluster and enzyme-bound MeCbl cofactor utilized during catalysis. UV-vis spectra and HPLC analysis indicated that SAM was transferring a methyl group to cobalamin(i), regenerating MeCbl. A QCMT reaction with a peptide substrate revealed the presence of two co-products, 5′-deoxyadenosine and
                      - emphasis [ref=e723]: S
                      - text: "-adenosyl-l-homocysteine."
                      - superscript [ref=e724]:
                        - link "39" [ref=e725] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit39"
                      - text: Due to the significant similarities in QCMT and RCMT, it is assumed that they catalyze similar reactions to methylate their corresponding residues.
                  - generic [ref=e726]:
                    - heading "2.1.5. Kynurenine formation from tryptophan" [level=4] [ref=e727]
                    - paragraph [ref=e728]:
                      - text: Methanobactins (Mbns) are ribosomally synthesized and posttranslationally modified natural products with a high affinity for Cu(i), utilized by methanotrophs as a copper source.
                      - superscript [ref=e729]:
                        - link "92" [ref=e730] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit92"
                      - text: While many proteins encoded by Mbn operons have assigned functions, the roles of MbnP, a tryptophan-rich protein, and MbnH, a diheme cytochrome
                      - emphasis [ref=e731]: c
                      - text: peroxidase (C
                      - emphasis [ref=e732]: c
                      - text: P), remained unclear until recently.
                      - superscript [ref=e733]:
                        - link "57,92" [ref=e734] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit57"
                      - text: MbnH belongs to the (bC
                      - emphasis [ref=e735]: c
                      - text: P)/MauG family, of which MauG catalyzes tryptophan tryptophylquinone (TTQ) formation in pre-methylamine dehydrogenase (preMADH)
                      - superscript [ref=e736]:
                        - link "93–95" [ref=e737] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit93"
                      - emphasis [ref=e738]: via
                      - text: a free radical approach
                      - superscript [ref=e739]:
                        - link "96,97" [ref=e740] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit96"
                      - text: mediated by a reactive
                      - emphasis [ref=e741]: bis
                      - text: "-Fe(iv) intermediate"
                      - superscript [ref=e742]:
                        - link "98" [ref=e743] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit98"
                      - text: in long-range remote catalysis (which will be discussed in Section 3.1).
                      - superscript [ref=e744]:
                        - link "96,99–102" [ref=e745] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit96"
                      - text: Notably, MbnH exhibits a charge resonance (CR) near-infrared band upon reaction with hydrogen peroxide, a spectroscopic signature associated with the
                      - emphasis [ref=e746]: bis
                      - text: "-Fe(iv) intermediate of MauG, indicating a through-space stabilization of the high-valent"
                      - emphasis [ref=e747]: bis
                      - text: "-Fe(iv) centers."
                      - superscript [ref=e748]:
                        - link "99,100,103,104" [ref=e749] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit99"
                      - text: A 2.09 Å resolution crystal structure of MbnP
                      - superscript [ref=e750]: H
                      - text: ", (MbnP coexpressed with MbnH), revealed a copper-binding site containing histidine, methionine, solvent-derived ligand, and kynurenine at residue 174 (Kyn174), a tryptophan-derived cofactor generated by oxygenation of a tryptophan residue ("
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e751] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig1"
                      - text: ", 15)."
                      - superscript [ref=e752]:
                        - link "57" [ref=e753] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit57"
                      - text: Kyn174 is a protein-bound residue, distinct from endogenous kynurenine that is present in the body as a metabolic intermediate in the major catabolic pathway of tryptophan.
                      - superscript [ref=e754]:
                        - link "100,105–108" [ref=e755] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit100"
                      - text: Trp174, the precursor to Kyn174, is part of a conserved WxW motif in MbnP proteins.
                      - superscript [ref=e756]:
                        - link "57" [ref=e757] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit57"
                      - text: Mutation of Trp174 to tyrosine significantly reduced copper binding, demonstrating the importance of the kynurenine cofactor for copper association.
                      - superscript [ref=e758]:
                        - link "57" [ref=e759] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit57"
                      - text: Although kynurenine is also present in the copper-binding sites of MopE* and CorA, these proteins adopt distinct structural folds (
                      - link "Fig. 6" [ref=e760] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig6"
                      - text: ).
                      - superscript [ref=e761]:
                        - link "57–59" [ref=e762] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit57"
                      - text: In all three proteins, the copper–kynurenine distance exceeds typical Cu–N bond lengths, suggesting that kynurenine is not directly coordinated with the copper ion.
                      - superscript [ref=e763]:
                        - link "57" [ref=e764] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit57"
                      - text: Further studies are needed to elucidate the mechanism of kynurenine formation from a tryptophan residue and the biological function of these proteins.
                    - figure [ref=e765]:
                      - heading "Fig. 6. Active sites of copper-binding proteins containing kynurenine. (A) The copper ion in MbnPH (light green; PDB 7L6G) is coordinated in a tetrahedral geometry by a methionine, histidine, kynurenine, and a water molecule. (B) The copper ion in MopE* (turquoise; PDB 2VOV) is coordinated by two histidine residues, kynurenine and a water molecule. (C) CorA's copper ion (pink; PDB 4BZ4) is also coordinated by two histidine residues, kynurenine, and a water molecule." [level=5] [ref=e766]:
                        - text: Fig. 6. Active sites of copper-binding proteins containing kynurenine. (A) The copper ion in MbnP
                        - superscript [ref=e767]: H
                        - text: (light green; PDB 7L6G) is coordinated in a tetrahedral geometry by a methionine, histidine, kynurenine, and a water molecule. (B) The copper ion in MopE* (turquoise; PDB 2VOV) is coordinated by two histidine residues, kynurenine and a water molecule. (C) CorA's copper ion (pink; PDB 4BZ4) is also coordinated by two histidine residues, kynurenine, and a water molecule.
                      - paragraph [ref=e768]:
                        - link "Fig. 6 🔍" [ref=e769] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f6.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 6" [ref=e770]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e772] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/fig6/
                  - generic [ref=e773]:
                    - 'heading "2.1.6. Tyrosine modification in copper amine oxidases: 2,4,5-trihydroxyphenylalanine quinone (TPQ) and lysine tyrosylquinone (LTQ)" [level=4] [ref=e774]'
                    - paragraph [ref=e775]:
                      - text: Copper amine oxidases (CuAOs) are homodimeric enzymes that catalyze the O
                      - subscript [ref=e776]: "2"
                      - text: "-dependent oxidation of primary amines to aldehydes, with the concomitant production of ammonia and H"
                      - subscript [ref=e777]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e778]: "2"
                      - text: through a ping-pong kinetic mechanism.
                      - superscript [ref=e779]:
                        - link "109" [ref=e780] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit109"
                      - text: These enzymes are widespread, participating in diverse biological processes such as cell growth, apoptosis, and hormone biosynthesis.
                      - superscript [ref=e781]:
                        - link "109" [ref=e782] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit109"
                      - text: Known as quinoproteins, CuAOs utilize covalently linked cofactors—2,4,5-trihydroxyphenylalanine quinone (TPQ) (
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e783] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig1"
                      - text: ", 16) or lysine tyrosylquinone (LTQ) ("
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e784] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig1"
                      - text: ", 26)—to facilitate catalysis. These quinone moieties are derived from tyrosine residues through an autocatalytic process that depends on copper and molecular oxygen."
                      - superscript [ref=e785]:
                        - link "60,110" [ref=e786] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit60"
                    - paragraph [ref=e787]:
                      - text: The TPQ cofactor was first identified in 1990
                      - emphasis [ref=e788]: via
                      - text: biochemical studies and later confirmed by X-ray crystallography (
                      - link "Fig. 7A" [ref=e789] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig7"
                      - text: ). Structural insights have been obtained from bacteria, plant, and mammal CuAOs, providing a comprehensive understanding of their catalytic mechanisms.
                      - superscript [ref=e790]:
                        - link "111–117" [ref=e791] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit111"
                      - text: Notably, the copper amine oxidase from
                      - emphasis [ref=e792]: Arthrobacter globiformis
                      - text: (AGAO) has been extensively studied to elucidate the stepwise formation of TPQ. Apo-AGAO crystals which feature an unmodified tyrosine residue Tyr382, were soaked in CuSO
                      - subscript [ref=e793]: "4"
                      - text: and exposed to an oxygen-saturated buffer to monitor TPQ (
                      - link "Fig. 8" [ref=e794] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig8"
                      - text: ).
                      - superscript [ref=e795]:
                        - link "118" [ref=e796] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit118"
                    - figure [ref=e797]:
                      - heading "Fig. 7. Crystal structures of cofactors in present in quinoproteins. (A) Amine oxidase of A. globiformis with mature TPQ cofactor (yellow; PDB 1IVX). The copper center is coordinated in a distorted square-pyramidal geometry by two water molecules and three histidine residues. (B) Wild-type MauG in complex with pre-MADH crystals aged 130 days, showing the crosslink between β-Trp57 and β-Trp108 (green; PDB 4FA1). (C) CTQ in the γ-subunit of QHNDH from Pseudomonas putida (blue; PDB 1JMX). Cys7, Cys27, and Cys41 of the γ-subunit are involved in thioether crosslinks with Asp or Glu residues that surround the CTQ cofactor." [level=5] [ref=e798]:
                        - text: Fig. 7. Crystal structures of cofactors in present in quinoproteins. (A) Amine oxidase of
                        - emphasis [ref=e799]: A. globiformis
                        - text: with mature TPQ cofactor (yellow; PDB 1IVX). The copper center is coordinated in a distorted square-pyramidal geometry by two water molecules and three histidine residues. (B) Wild-type MauG in complex with pre-MADH crystals aged 130 days, showing the crosslink between β-Trp57 and β-Trp108 (green; PDB 4FA1). (C) CTQ in the γ-subunit of QHNDH from
                        - emphasis [ref=e800]: Pseudomonas putida
                        - text: (blue; PDB 1JMX). Cys7, Cys27, and Cys41 of the γ-subunit are involved in thioether crosslinks with Asp or Glu residues that surround the CTQ cofactor.
                      - paragraph [ref=e801]:
                        - link "Fig. 7 🔍" [ref=e802] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f7.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 7" [ref=e803]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e805] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/fig7/
                    - figure [ref=e806]:
                      - heading "Fig. 8. Proposed mechanism of formation of TPQ in AGAO.118 Crystal structures for apo-AGAO, CuII-AGAO, DPQ, TPQred, and TPQox have been characterized and published with PDB codes 1AVK, 1IVU, 1IVV, 1IVW, and 1IVX, respectively." [level=5] [ref=e807]:
                        - text: Fig. 8. Proposed mechanism of formation of TPQ in AGAO.
                        - superscript [ref=e808]:
                          - link "118" [ref=e809] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#cit118"
                        - text: Crystal structures for apo-AGAO, Cu
                        - superscript [ref=e810]: II
                        - text: "-AGAO, DPQ, TPQ"
                        - subscript [ref=e811]: red
                        - text: ", and TPQ"
                        - subscript [ref=e812]: ox
                        - text: have been characterized and published with PDB codes 1AVK, 1IVU, 1IVV, 1IVW, and 1IVX, respectively.
                      - paragraph [ref=e813]:
                        - link "Fig. 8 🔍" [ref=e814] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f8.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 8" [ref=e815]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e817] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/fig8/
                    - paragraph [ref=e818]:
                      - text: The first step in TPQ formation involves the binding of a copper(ii) ion to the protein, positioning it approximately 2.5 Å from the hydroxyl group of Tyr382. Upon exposure to oxygen for 10 minutes, an O atom was detected near the C3 position of the tyrosine ring, with an occupancy of 0.5.
                      - superscript [ref=e819]:
                        - link "118" [ref=e820] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit118"
                      - text: This observation suggested the presence of a transient intermediate comprising both the unmodified tyrosine and its partially oxidized quinone form, referred to as dopaquinone (DPQ).
                      - superscript [ref=e821]:
                        - link "118" [ref=e822] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit118"
                    - paragraph [ref=e823]:
                      - text: Prolonged exposure to oxygen for 100 minutes revealed another intermediate lacking the characteristic 480 nm absorption peak of fully mature TPQ. This intermediate exhibited two oxygen atoms near the C2 and C5 positions of the tyrosine ring, consistent with the reduced form of TPQ (TPQ
                      - subscript [ref=e824]: red
                      - text: ).
                      - superscript [ref=e825]:
                        - link "118" [ref=e826] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit118"
                      - text: Since mature TPQ contains oxygen atoms at the C2, C4, and C5 positions, the previously observed oxygen at C3 in DPQ likely corresponds to C5 in TPQ
                      - subscript [ref=e827]: red
                      - text: ", implying a 180° rotation of the quinone ring during maturation."
                    - paragraph [ref=e828]:
                      - text: The final step involves the oxidation of TPQ
                      - subscript [ref=e829]: red
                      - text: by a second molecule of molecular oxygen, yielding the fully mature oxidized form of TPQ (TPQ
                      - subscript [ref=e830]: ox
                      - text: ) and H
                      - subscript [ref=e831]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e832]: "2"
                      - text: .
                      - superscript [ref=e833]:
                        - link "118,119" [ref=e834] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit118"
                      - text: This sequential mechanism highlights the intricate interplay between copper coordination, tyrosine oxidation, and ring conformational changes during TPQ biogenesis.
                    - paragraph [ref=e835]: By contrast, LTQ biogenesis involves a crosslink between lysine and tyrosine residues, forming a distinct quinone cofactor, which will be further discussed in Section 3.3. Although less studied than TPQ, LTQ plays a similarly essential role in enabling CuAO catalysis and warrants further exploration to delineate its formation and function in enzymatic systems.
                    - paragraph [ref=e836]:
                      - text: The catalytic mechanism of CuAOs has been studied, revealing a two-step process comprising a reductive half-reaction and an oxidative half-reaction (
                      - link "Fig. 9" [ref=e837] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig9"
                      - text: ). In the reductive phase, the primary amine substrate is oxidized to an aldehyde, while TPQ is reduced. The oxidative half-reaction regenerates TPQ, releasing NH
                      - subscript [ref=e838]: "3"
                      - text: and H
                      - subscript [ref=e839]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e840]: "2"
                      - text: as byproducts.
                      - superscript [ref=e841]:
                        - link "120" [ref=e842] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit120"
                    - figure [ref=e843]:
                      - heading "Fig. 9. Proposed catalytic mechanism of CuAOs. Steps 1–4 belong to the reductive half-reaction and the remaining steps are the oxidative half-reaction.120 TPQox, fully oxidized TPQ; TPQssb, TPQ substrate Schiff-base; TPQpsb, TPQ product Schiff-base; TPQamr, TPQ aminoresorcinol form; TPQsq, TPQ semiquinone radical, the “on-copper” catalytically active conformation, TPQ semiquinone form; TPQimq, TPQ iminoquinone form." [level=5] [ref=e844]:
                        - text: Fig. 9. Proposed catalytic mechanism of CuAOs. Steps 1–4 belong to the reductive half-reaction and the remaining steps are the oxidative half-reaction.
                        - superscript [ref=e845]:
                          - link "120" [ref=e846] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#cit120"
                        - text: TPQ
                        - subscript [ref=e847]: ox
                        - text: ", fully oxidized TPQ; TPQ"
                        - subscript [ref=e848]: ssb
                        - text: ", TPQ substrate Schiff-base; TPQ"
                        - subscript [ref=e849]: psb
                        - text: ", TPQ product Schiff-base; TPQ"
                        - subscript [ref=e850]: amr
                        - text: ", TPQ aminoresorcinol form; TPQ"
                        - subscript [ref=e851]: sq
                        - text: ", TPQ semiquinone radical, the “on-copper” catalytically active conformation, TPQ semiquinone form; TPQ"
                        - subscript [ref=e852]: imq
                        - text: ", TPQ iminoquinone form."
                      - paragraph [ref=e853]:
                        - link "Fig. 9 🔍" [ref=e854] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f9.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 9" [ref=e855]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e857] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/fig9/
                    - paragraph [ref=e858]:
                      - text: A recent study provided new insights into the dynamic conformational changes during catalysis through
                      - emphasis [ref=e859]: in crystallo
                      - text: thermodynamic analysis of noncryocooled AGAO crystals.
                      - superscript [ref=e860]:
                        - link "120" [ref=e861] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit120"
                      - text: "TPQ exists in two major conformations: the “off-copper” state, where it is distant from the copper(ii) ion, and “on-copper” state, where it coordinates with the metal center and is catalytically active. During the reductive half-reaction, TPQ is reduced by the substrate to its aminoresorcinol form (TPQ"
                      - subscript [ref=e862]: amr
                      - text: ) which resides in the off-copper state. This form is in equilibrium with the on-copper semiquinone radical (TPQ
                      - subscript [ref=e863]: sq
                      - text: ).
                      - superscript [ref=e864]:
                        - link "120" [ref=e865] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit120"
                    - paragraph [ref=e866]:
                      - text: Murakawa and colleagues ingeniously employed the humid air and glue-coating (HAG) method, commonly used in noncryogenic crystallography, to investigate the temperature-dependent behavior of TPQ. Using anaerobically substrate-soaked AGAO crystals, they demonstrate that increasing temperature facilitates the transition from TPQ
                      - subscript [ref=e867]: amr
                      - text: to TPQ
                      - subscript [ref=e868]: sq
                      - text: . This transition was further corroborated by single-crystal UV-vis spectroscopy, providing direct evidence of temperature-mediated conformational equilibrium.
                      - superscript [ref=e869]:
                        - link "98" [ref=e870] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit98"
                      - text: These findings emphasize the sophisticated interplay of cofactor conformation, substrate interactions, and environmental conditions in CuAO catalysis, offering deeper insights into the mechanistic versatility of protein-derived cofactors.
                - generic [ref=e871]:
                  - 'heading "2.2. One oxidation state up: single amino acid residues harboring paramagnetic free radicals as cofactors" [level=3] [ref=e872]'
                  - paragraph [ref=e873]: Free radicals on amino acid residues represent a unique class of protein-derived cofactors, serving as catalytic driving forces in diverse enzymatic systems. This section explores the biogenesis, stabilization, and functional roles of these radicals.
                  - generic [ref=e874]:
                    - heading "2.2.1. Stable tyrosyl radical" [level=4] [ref=e875]
                    - paragraph [ref=e876]:
                      - text: The discovery of a stable tyrosyl radical in
                      - emphasis [ref=e877]: Escherichia coli
                      - text: ribonucleotide reductase (RNR) revolutionized the understanding of free radicals in enzymology, demonstrating that radicals can be both highly stable and integral to catalysis. First identified in the R2 subunit in 1972 as a built-in organic radical required for enzyme activity with a sharp absorbance at 410 nm and a doublet splitting EPR signal at
                      - emphasis [ref=e878]: g
                      - text: = 2.0047,
                      - superscript [ref=e879]:
                        - link "121" [ref=e880] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit121"
                      - text: a stable tyrosyl free radical cofactor in the protein was discovered,
                      - superscript [ref=e881]:
                        - link "122–124" [ref=e882] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit122"
                      - text: expanding the field of radical enzymology.
                      - superscript [ref=e883]:
                        - link "124–126" [ref=e884] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit124"
                      - text: The tyrosyl radical has remarkable stability, persisting for days at room temperature if the protein remains intact.
                      - superscript [ref=e885]:
                        - link "123" [ref=e886] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit123"
                    - paragraph [ref=e887]:
                      - text: This radical is formed during protein synthesis
                      - emphasis [ref=e888]: via
                      - text: an autocatalytic process involving a diiron cluster. Oxygen reacts with the diiron center to form a mixed-valence Fe(iii)–O–Fe(iv) intermediate,
                      - superscript [ref=e889]:
                        - link "127–129" [ref=e890] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit127"
                      - text: which oxidizes a nearby tyrosine residue, yielding the tyrosyl radical while reducing the diiron cluster to the diferric state. The hydrophobic pocket surrounding the diiron center and the local electrostatic environment provide a protective environment that stabilizes the radical.
                      - superscript [ref=e891]:
                        - link "130,131" [ref=e892] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit130"
                    - paragraph [ref=e893]:
                      - text: The catalytic role of the tyrosyl radical becomes evident upon interaction with the R1 subunit. When R1 binds its substrate, a ribonucleotide diphosphate, the two subunits form a complex,
                      - superscript [ref=e894]:
                        - link "132" [ref=e895] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit132"
                      - text: enabling the transfer of oxidizing power from the R2 subunit's tyrosyl radical through a hydrogen-bonded network to the active site in R1. This generates a cysteine thiol radical that initiates substrate reduction.
                      - superscript [ref=e896]:
                        - link "133,134" [ref=e897] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit133"
                      - text: Following each turnover, the tyrosyl radical is regenerated, demonstrating its role as a true catalytic cofactor.
                      - superscript [ref=e898]:
                        - link "135" [ref=e899] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit135"
                    - paragraph [ref=e900]:
                      - text: Beyond RNRs, tyrosyl radicals as protein-derived cofactors play significant roles in other systems, such as photosystem II (PSII) and prostaglandin H synthase (PGHS).
                      - superscript [ref=e901]:
                        - link "136" [ref=e902] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit136"
                      - text: In PSII, two tyrosyl radicals, Y
                      - subscript [ref=e903]: Z
                      - text: ˙ and Y
                      - subscript [ref=e904]: D
                      - text: ˙, contribute distinct functions in water-splitting catalysis. Y
                      - subscript [ref=e905]: Z
                      - text: ˙ mediates electron transfer to P680
                      - superscript [ref=e906]: +
                      - text: in the photosynthetic electron transport chain, rapidly reduced back to tyrosine and oxidized to the radical state. Conversely, Y
                      - subscript [ref=e907]: D
                      - text: ˙ is exceptionally stable, persisting for minutes to hours, and is believed to assist in assembling and stabilizing the oxygen-evolving complex.
                      - superscript [ref=e908]:
                        - link "137,138" [ref=e909] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit137"
                      - text: Computational studies have revealed that Y
                      - subscript [ref=e910]: D
                      - text: ˙'s stability is linked to a water-mediated proton transfer pathway within its hydrophobic environment, explaining its slower oxidation kinetics.
                      - superscript [ref=e911]:
                        - link "139" [ref=e912] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit139"
                      - text: PGHS is a heme-dependent bifunctional enzyme, a tyrosyl radical plays an active role in the cyclooxygenase activity that initiates prostaglandin biosynthesis.
                      - superscript [ref=e913]:
                        - link "140–142" [ref=e914] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit140"
                      - text: The radical is generated during catalysis and accumulates alongside product formation, signifying its central role in the reaction mechanism. These examples underscore the versatility and adaptability of tyrosyl radicals as catalytic cofactors in diverse enzymatic systems.
                  - generic [ref=e915]:
                    - heading "2.2.2. Stable glycyl radical" [level=4] [ref=e916]
                    - paragraph [ref=e917]:
                      - text: Glycyl radicals are critical to the catalytic activity of enzymes involved in anaerobic metabolism, such as pyruvate formate lyase (PFL) and anaerobic RNRs (class III RNRs).
                      - superscript [ref=e918]:
                        - link "143–148" [ref=e919] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit143"
                      - text: These radicals are generated by activating enzymes (termed activases) containing Fe–S clusters, which produce a 5′-deoxyadenosyl radical from
                      - emphasis [ref=e920]: S
                      - text: "-adenosyl methionine."
                      - superscript [ref=e921]:
                        - link "149" [ref=e922] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit149"
                      - text: A Zn(Cys)
                      - subscript [ref=e923]: "4"
                      - text: center is present in the catalytic subunit of anaerobic RNR, and its absence through mutation is shown to inactivate the enzyme as the result of their inability to generate the catalytically essential glycyl radical although the mechanism of which requires further investigation.
                      - superscript [ref=e924]:
                        - link "150" [ref=e925] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit150"
                      - text: This radical abstracts a hydrogen atom from a glycine residue, creating a catalytically active glycyl radical.
                      - superscript [ref=e926]:
                        - link "151–153" [ref=e927] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit151"
                      - text: Glycyl radicals in these enzymes have demonstrated permanent stability under strict anaerobic conditions.
                      - superscript [ref=e928]:
                        - link "147,148" [ref=e929] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit147"
                      - text: However, glycyl radicals are extremely oxygen-sensitive, resulting in peptide cleavage at the radical site and enzyme inactivation upon exposure.
                      - superscript [ref=e930]:
                        - link "41,146,154" [ref=e931] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit41"
                      - text: In PFL and class III RNR, the glycyl radical is transferred to a cysteine residue, generating a catalytic thiyl radical essential for pyruvate cleavage or nucleotide diphosphate reduction.
                      - superscript [ref=e932]:
                        - link "40,153,155" [ref=e933] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit40"
                    - paragraph [ref=e934]:
                      - text: Other enzymes containing glycyl radicals include glycerol dehydratase, benzylsuccinate synthase, and hydroxyphenylacetate decarboxylase.
                      - superscript [ref=e935]:
                        - link "153" [ref=e936] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit153"
                      - text: Collectively termed glycyl radical enzymes (GREs), they share a common mechanism of radical transfer to cysteine residues for catalytic activity. Stabilization and precise transfer mechanisms of glycyl radicals remain active areas of research.
                  - generic [ref=e937]:
                    - heading "2.2.3. Transient thiyl and tryptophanyl radicals" [level=4] [ref=e938]
                    - paragraph [ref=e939]:
                      - text: It is debatable whether transient amino acid radicals mediating catalysis should be formally classified as protein-derived cofactors. While they are crucial for enzyme function and are generated within the protein itself, their transient nature distinguishes them from more stable protein-derived cofactors like permanently stable tyrosyl radicals in RNR or PSII. Therefore, while these radicals are important for enzyme catalysis and should be discussed, they may not always be explicitly listed alongside other, more stable protein-derived cofactors in a comprehensive overview such as
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e940] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig1"
                      - text: .
                    - paragraph [ref=e941]:
                      - text: Thiyl radicals are essential to the catalytic mechanisms of RNRs, which play a central role in DNA synthesis. All RNR classes employ a transient thiyl radical to initiate nucleotide reduction, with variations in its generation mechanisms.
                      - superscript [ref=e942]:
                        - link "144,156–163" [ref=e943] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit144"
                      - text: To kinetically and spectroscopically study the elusive thiyl radical, researchers have utilized selenocysteine substitution.
                      - superscript [ref=e944]:
                        - link "164" [ref=e945] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit164"
                      - text: With its lower p
                      - emphasis [ref=e946]: K
                      - subscript [ref=e947]: a
                      - text: and reduction potential, selenocysteine enhances radical stabilization, facilitating detection and advancing the understanding of thiyl radicals in enzymatic catalysis.
                      - superscript [ref=e948]:
                        - link "165" [ref=e949] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit165"
                    - paragraph [ref=e950]:
                      - text: Tryptophan radical cations are exemplified by cytochrome
                      - emphasis [ref=e951]: c
                      - text: peroxidase (C
                      - emphasis [ref=e952]: c
                      - text: P), a heme-dependent enzyme that catalyzes the oxidation of Fe(ii)-cytochrome
                      - emphasis [ref=e953]: c
                      - text: to Fe(iii)-cytochrome
                      - emphasis [ref=e954]: c
                      - text: while reducing H
                      - subscript [ref=e955]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e956]: "2"
                      - text: to water.
                      - superscript [ref=e957]:
                        - link "166" [ref=e958] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit166"
                      - text: The key intermediate is Trp191˙
                      - superscript [ref=e959]: +
                      - text: ","
                      - superscript [ref=e960]:
                        - link "167" [ref=e961] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit167"
                      - text: which facilitates long-range electron transfer to its substrate, cytochrome
                      - emphasis [ref=e962]: c
                      - text: .
                      - superscript [ref=e963]:
                        - link "168–170" [ref=e964] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit168"
                      - text: Structural and spectroscopic studies reveal that protein environments, such as solvent accessibility and proximal charges, critically influence the stability of Trp˙
                      - superscript [ref=e965]: +
                      - text: .
                      - superscript [ref=e966]:
                        - link "171" [ref=e967] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit171"
                      - text: For example, the presence of a nearby cation-binding site in ascorbate peroxidase (APX) leads to the stabilization of a porphyrin π-cation radical instead of a Trp˙
                      - superscript [ref=e968]: +
                      - text: .
                      - superscript [ref=e969]:
                        - link "172,173" [ref=e970] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit172"
                      - text: However, in
                      - emphasis [ref=e971]: Leishmania major
                      - text: C
                      - emphasis [ref=e972]: c
                      - text: P (LmP), a Trp˙
                      - superscript [ref=e973]: +
                      - text: radical forms despite the presence of a similar cation-binding site, suggesting that solvent environment differences may play a significant role.
                      - superscript [ref=e974]:
                        - link "174" [ref=e975] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit174"
                      - text: Recent computational studies have investigated the factors governing Trp˙
                      - superscript [ref=e976]: +
                      - text: stabilization, highlighting the roles of protein structure and solvent interactions.
                      - superscript [ref=e977]:
                        - link "175" [ref=e978] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit175"
                      - text: Despite substantial progress, questions remain about why C
                      - emphasis [ref=e979]: c
                      - text: P favors a Trp˙
                      - superscript [ref=e980]: +
                      - text: over a porphyrin radical and how this radical is reduced during catalysis.
              - generic [ref=e981]:
                - heading "3. Protein cofactors derived from the covalent crosslink of two amino acids" [level=2] [ref=e982]: 3. Protein cofactors derived from the covalent crosslink of two amino acids
                - paragraph [ref=e983]:
                  - text: Covalent crosslinking between two amino acid residues forms a unique class of protein-derived cofactors, which play crucial roles in enhancing enzyme catalysis by creating specialized active sites with new redox centers. Among these cofactors, eight distinct types have been characterized to date, including Cys–His, Cys–Lys, Cys–Tyr, Cys–Trp, His–Tyr, Lys–Tyr, oxygenated Trp–Trp, and the radical form of (Cys–Tyr)˙ (
                  - link "Table 2" [ref=e984] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tab2"
                  - text: ). These crosslinked cofactors exhibit unique structural and functional properties, often arising through intricate biosynthetic pathways. This section examines these cofactors in detail, with a focus on their structural features, catalytic roles, and the underlying mechanisms of their formation.
                - generic [ref=e985]:
                  - heading "Table 2. Protein cofactors derived from the covalent crosslink of two amino acids." [level=3] [ref=e986]

                        - cell "12" [ref=e1083]:

                      - row "Cys–Tyr Copper radical oxidase (GlxA) Autocatalytic Catalysis 189" [ref=e1085]:

                      - row "Cys–Tyr Sulfite reductase (NirA) Autocatalytic Catalysis 190" [ref=e1092]:

                      - row "Cys–Tyr Putative zinc protease (BF4112) Autocatalytic Catalysis (putative) 15" [ref=e1099]:
