                        - cell "15" [ref=e1104]:
                          - link "15" [ref=e1105] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#cit15"
                      - row "Cys–Tyr Cytochrome c nitrite reductase (TvNiR) Autocatalytic Catalysis 191" [ref=e1106]:
                        - cell "Cys–Tyr" [ref=e1107]
                        - cell "Cytochrome c nitrite reductase (TvNiR)" [ref=e1108]:
                          - text: Cytochrome
                          - emphasis [ref=e1109]: c
                          - text: nitrite reductase (TvNiR)
                        - cell "Autocatalytic" [ref=e1110]
                        - cell "Catalysis" [ref=e1111]
                        - cell "191" [ref=e1112]:
                          - link "191" [ref=e1113] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#cit191"
                      - row "His–Tyr His–Tyr (a) aromatic C–N Cytochrome c oxidase Autocatalytic Copper binding 192–194" [ref=e1114]:
                        - cell "His–Tyr" [ref=e1115]
                        - cell "His–Tyr (a)" [ref=e1116]
                        - cell "aromatic C–N" [ref=e1117]
                        - cell "Cytochrome c oxidase" [ref=e1118]:
                          - text: Cytochrome
                          - emphasis [ref=e1119]: c
                          - text: oxidase
                        - cell "Autocatalytic" [ref=e1120]
                        - cell "Copper binding" [ref=e1121]
                        - cell "192–194" [ref=e1122]:
                          - link "192–194" [ref=e1123] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#cit192"
                      - row "His–Tyr (b) Cβ–N Catalase TBD TBD 195 and 196" [ref=e1124]:
                        - cell "His–Tyr (b)" [ref=e1125]
                        - cell "Cβ–N" [ref=e1126]:
                          - text: C
                          - subscript [ref=e1127]: β
                          - text: –N
                        - cell "Catalase" [ref=e1128]
                        - cell "TBD" [ref=e1129]
                        - cell "TBD" [ref=e1130]
                        - cell "195 and 196" [ref=e1131]:
                          - link "195 and 196" [ref=e1132] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#cit195"
                      - row "Lys–Tyr LTQ C–N Lysyl oxidase Autocatalytic Catalysis 110" [ref=e1133]:
                        - cell "Lys–Tyr" [ref=e1134]
                        - cell "LTQ" [ref=e1135]
                        - cell "C–N" [ref=e1136]
                        - cell "Lysyl oxidase" [ref=e1137]
                        - cell "Autocatalytic" [ref=e1138]
                        - cell "Catalysis" [ref=e1139]
                        - cell "110" [ref=e1140]:
                          - link "110" [ref=e1141] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#cit110"
                      - row "Trp–Trp TTQ C–C Methylamine dehydrogenase (MADH) Enzymatic (MauG) Catalysis 197–199" [ref=e1142]:
                        - cell "Trp–Trp" [ref=e1143]
                        - cell "TTQ" [ref=e1144]
                        - cell "C–C" [ref=e1145]
                        - cell "Methylamine dehydrogenase (MADH)" [ref=e1146]
                        - cell "Enzymatic (MauG)" [ref=e1147]
                        - cell "Catalysis" [ref=e1148]
                        - cell "197–199" [ref=e1149]:
                          - link "197–199" [ref=e1150] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#cit197"
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1152] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: table/tab2/
                - generic [ref=e1153]:
                  - 'heading "3.1. Tryptophan modification in amine dehydrogenases: tryptophan tryptophylquinone (TTQ)" [level=3] [ref=e1154]'
                  - paragraph [ref=e1155]:
                    - text: The protein-derived cofactors accommodated by quinoproteins are among the most well-studied in terms of mechanisms of formation. Currently, five amino acid crosslinked cofactors with quinone moieties have been identified as redox centers in enzymes.
                    - superscript [ref=e1156]:
                      - link "22" [ref=e1157] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit22"
                    - text: "These are:"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1158]:
                    - text: • Cysteine tryptophylquinone (CTQ,
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1159] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 25) in quinohemoprotein amine dehydrogenase (QHNDH),"
                    - superscript [ref=e1160]:
                      - link "60,110,183,197,200,201" [ref=e1161] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit60"
                    - text: l-lysine ε-oxidase (LodA),
                    - superscript [ref=e1162]:
                      - link "202" [ref=e1163] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit202"
                    - text: and glycine oxidase (GoxA).
                    - superscript [ref=e1164]:
                      - link "185" [ref=e1165] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit185"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1166]:
                    - text: • Lysine tyrosylquinone (LTQ,
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1167] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 26) in lysyl oxidase."
                    - superscript [ref=e1168]:
                      - link "110" [ref=e1169] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit110"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1170]:
                    - text: • Pyrroloquinoline quinone (PQQ, 37 in
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1171] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 37)"
                    - superscript [ref=e1172]:
                      - link "203" [ref=e1173] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit203"
                    - text: in alcohol dehydrogenases and aminoadipic 6-semialdehyde dehydrogenase.
                    - superscript [ref=e1174]:
                      - link "204,205" [ref=e1175] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit204"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1176]:
                    - text: • 2,4,5-Trihydroxyphenylalanine quinone (TPQ,
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1177] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 16) in amine oxidases."
                    - superscript [ref=e1178]:
                      - link "60" [ref=e1179] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit60"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1180]:
                    - text: • Tryptophan tryptophylquinone (TTQ,
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1181] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 24) in methylamine dehydrogenase."
                    - superscript [ref=e1182]:
                      - link "197–199" [ref=e1183] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit197"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1184]:
                    - text: Among them, the biogenesis of TTQ is well studied. This quinone cofactor formed through the crosslinking and subsequent oxygenation of two tryptophan components (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1185] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 24). TTQ is the hallmark cofactor of methylamine dehydrogenase (MADH), an enzyme that catalyzes the oxidation of methylamine to formaldehyde and ammonia."
                    - superscript [ref=e1186]:
                      - link "197" [ref=e1187] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit197"
                    - text: TTQ serves as a prime example of how protein-derived cofactors confer remarkable catalytic capabilities, enhancing substrate specificity and turnover efficiency. The formation of TTQ involves a sophisticated posttranslational modification pathway, mediated by the diheme enzyme MauG.
                    - superscript [ref=e1188]:
                      - link "206,207" [ref=e1189] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit206"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1190]:
                    - text: MauG must handle a large substrate with specificity. The biogenesis of TTQ is accomplished in a 119 kDa cofactor-free precursor protein, preMADH, containing two tryptophan residues at positions critical for cofactor assembly.
                    - superscript [ref=e1191]:
                      - link "93" [ref=e1192] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit93"
                    - text: MauG catalyzes the oxidative maturation of these residues into TTQ
                    - emphasis [ref=e1193]: via
                    - text: a six-electron oxidation process (
                    - link "Fig. 10" [ref=e1194] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig10"
                    - text: ).
                    - superscript [ref=e1195]:
                      - link "97,102" [ref=e1196] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit97"
                    - text: The oxidant in this system, H
                    - subscript [ref=e1197]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1198]: "2"
                    - text: ", interacts with the deeply buried hemes of MauG, initiating a cascade of high-energy intermediates."
                  - figure [ref=e1199]:
                    - heading "Fig. 10. Proposed biogenesis mechanism of TTQ in MADH.96 TTQ maturation in MauG-preMADH crystals with slow release of H2O2 of the cryoprotectant illustrates TTQ formation, showing that the crosslink between the two Trp residues occurs prior to the addition of a second oxygen atom. The PDB codes for aged crystal structures are 4FA4, 4FA5, 4FA9, 4FAN, 4FAV, and 4FA1, in ascending order." [level=4] [ref=e1200]:
                      - text: Fig. 10. Proposed biogenesis mechanism of TTQ in MADH.
                      - superscript [ref=e1201]:
                        - link "96" [ref=e1202] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit96"
                      - text: TTQ maturation in MauG-preMADH crystals with slow release of H
                      - subscript [ref=e1203]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e1204]: "2"
                      - text: of the cryoprotectant illustrates TTQ formation, showing that the crosslink between the two Trp residues occurs prior to the addition of a second oxygen atom. The PDB codes for aged crystal structures are 4FA4, 4FA5, 4FA9, 4FAN, 4FAV, and 4FA1, in ascending order.
                    - paragraph [ref=e1205]:
                      - link "Fig. 10 🔍" [ref=e1206] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f10.jpg
                        - img "Fig. 10" [ref=e1207]
                        - text: 🔍
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1209] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/fig10/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1210]:
                    - text: Upon binding H
                    - subscript [ref=e1211]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1212]: "2"
                    - text: ", the distal heme of MauG undergoes oxidation to form an Fe"
                    - generic "DOUBLE BOND, LENGTH AS M-DASH" [ref=e1213]:
                      - img [ref=e1214]
                    - text: O species coupled with a porphyrin radical. The oxidizing equivalent is then transferred to the proximal heme, generating a
                    - emphasis [ref=e1217]: bis
                    - text: "-Fe(iv) intermediate."
                    - superscript [ref=e1218]:
                      - link "98" [ref=e1219] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit98"
                    - text: This
                    - emphasis [ref=e1220]: bis
                    - text: "-Fe(iv) species is remarkably stable in the absence of preMADH, persisting for hours due to a biological charge resonance (CR) stabilization mechanism."
                    - superscript [ref=e1221]:
                      - link "99,100" [ref=e1222] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit99"
                    - text: This stabilization is characterized by a near-infrared (NIR) absorption band with maxima at 950–960 nm, a phenomenon rarely observed in biological systems. Unlike the short-lived ferryl intermediates that typically oxidize small-molecular substrates, the exceptional stability of
                    - emphasis [ref=e1223]: bis
                    - text: "-Fe(iv) in 43 kDa MauG enables the intermediate to oxidize a large substrate of 119 kDa preMADH on its specific two tryptophan residues (one is an already hydroxylated 7-OH-Trp) through long-range remote catalysis."
                    - superscript [ref=e1224]:
                      - link "96,101" [ref=e1225] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit96"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1226]:
                    - text: The
                    - emphasis [ref=e1227]: bis
                    - text: "-Fe(iv) intermediate serves as the catalytic linchpin for oxidizing the tryptophan residues in preMADH."
                    - superscript [ref=e1228]:
                      - link "98" [ref=e1229] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit98"
                    - text: Despite the physical separation (∼40 Å) between MauG's catalytic center and the substrate tryptophans, the oxidation proceeds through a long-range electron transfer mechanism.
                    - superscript [ref=e1230]:
                      - link "102" [ref=e1231] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit102"
                    - text: This transfer is facilitated by a network of aromatic residues in MauG and involves a hole-hopping pathway
                    - emphasis [ref=e1232]: via
                    - text: a surface-exposed tryptophan residue.
                    - superscript [ref=e1233]:
                      - link "101" [ref=e1234] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit101"
                    - text: The oxidative modification of preMADH occurs across an approximately 40 Å interface between the H
                    - subscript [ref=e1235]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1236]: "2"
                    - text: "-binding site in MauG and the TTQ formation site in preMADH, producing a diradical of Trp˙ and (Trp-OH)˙ for crosslinking,"
                    - superscript [ref=e1237]:
                      - link "96" [ref=e1238] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit96"
                    - text: exemplifying the efficiency of biological electron transfer mechanisms.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1239]:
                    - text: Each TTQ assembly cycle involves the consumption of one H
                    - subscript [ref=e1240]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1241]: "2"
                    - text: molecule, repeating three times to deliver the six required oxidizing equivalents. During each cycle, MauG's
                    - emphasis [ref=e1242]: bis
                    - text: "-Fe(iv) intermediate oxidizes one target tryptophan residue in preMADH. The modifications include hydroxylation, crosslink formation, and further oxidation to achieve the mature TTQ structure."
                    - superscript [ref=e1243]:
                      - link "96" [ref=e1244] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit96"
                    - text: This cumulative oxidative process transforms preMADH into catalytically active MADH, equipped with a fully formed TTQ cofactor (
                    - link "Fig. 7B" [ref=e1245] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig7"
                    - text: ).
                - generic [ref=e1246]:
                  - heading "3.2. Cysteine tryptophylquinone (CTQ)" [level=3] [ref=e1247]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1248]:
                    - text: CTQ is a quinone cofactor derived from the crosslinking of cysteine and tryptophan residues (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1249] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 25)."
                    - superscript [ref=e1250]:
                      - link "208" [ref=e1251] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit208"
                    - text: It has been identified in various enzymes, including quinohemoprotein amine dehydrogenase (QHNDH)
                    - superscript [ref=e1252]:
                      - link "183" [ref=e1253] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit183"
                    - text: l-lysine ε-oxidase (LodA),
                    - superscript [ref=e1254]:
                      - link "202" [ref=e1255] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit202"
                    - text: and glycine oxidase (GoxA),
                    - superscript [ref=e1256]:
                      - link "185" [ref=e1257] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit185"
                    - text: where it plays a crucial role in the oxidation of primary amines or amino acids to their corresponding aldehydes or keto acids. These enzymatic reactions are significant in energy metabolism and catabolism, demonstrating the versatility and catalytic efficiency of CTQ-containing enzymes.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1258]:
                    - text: QHNDH is a diheme enzyme that catalyzes the oxidation of primary amines to aldehydes, with energy generation as a byproduct. Its CTQ cofactor is formed during a multistep biosynthetic process involving several auxiliary proteins. The flavoprotein monooxygenase QhpG plays a central role in catalyzing the final oxidation step to form the mature CTQ (
                    - link "Fig. 7C" [ref=e1259] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig7"
                    - text: ) from the precursor peptide encoded by the
                    - emphasis [ref=e1260]: qhpC
                    - text: gene, which forms the γ-subunit of QHNDH. However, this final step is preceded by sequential modifications orchestrated by QhpD and QhpE.
                    - superscript [ref=e1261]:
                      - link "209" [ref=e1262] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit209"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1263]: QhpD mediates the formation of three Cys-to-Asp/Glu thioether bonds within the QhpC polypeptide, a step critical for stabilizing the cofactor's nascent structure. Structural studies revealed that QhpG preferentially binds to a QhpCD binary complex, facilitating efficient CTQ formation. This hierarchical assembly and multienzyme coordination are reminiscent of TTQ biogenesis in methylamine dehydrogenase (MADH), which similarly involves a diheme enzyme (MauG) to oxidize precursor tryptophan residues.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1264]:
                    - text: In l-lysine ε-oxidase (LodA) and glycine oxidase (GoxA), CTQ synthesis occurs
                    - emphasis [ref=e1265]: via
                    - text: distinct pathways. Unlike the diheme protein-dependent biogenesis seen in QHNDH, LodA and GoxA rely on flavoproteins, LodB and GoxB, respectively, to generate their cofactors.
                    - superscript [ref=e1266]:
                      - link "184,185,202,210" [ref=e1267] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit184"
                    - text: These differences suggest unique evolutionary adaptations and mechanistic diversity among CTQ-bearing enzymes. Comparative studies have highlighted variations in the roles and structural contexts of CTQ within these systems, emphasizing the need for further research to delineate the precise biogenesis and catalytic mechanisms across different enzyme classes.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1268]:
                    - text: CTQ exemplifies the biochemical ingenuity of quinone cofactors in enzymology, showcasing the versatility of protein-derived modifications in facilitating challenging redox reactions. Its formation and function differ across enzyme families, illustrating how protein environments and accessory proteins adapt to specific biochemical needs. For a comprehensive overview of quinone cofactors, readers are directed to the detailed review by Klinman and Bonnot, which delves into the biogenesis and functionality of these remarkable molecular motifs.
                    - superscript [ref=e1269]:
                      - link "22" [ref=e1270] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit22"
                    - text: The formation and stabilization of these crosslinked quinone cofactors underscore the evolutionary creativity of enzymes in harnessing free radical chemistry and long-range electron transfer for catalysis. As research advances, further insights into the structural determinants and dynamic pathways governing CTQ and TTQ biogenesis will continue to inspire biomimetic and synthetic applications.
                - generic [ref=e1271]:
                  - heading "3.3. Lysine tyrosylquinone (LTQ)" [level=3] [ref=e1272]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1273]:
                    - text: The quinone moiety of LTQ is also derived from a tyrosine residue. However, in addition to the tyrosine modification, there is also a crosslink between the quinone tyrosine and the side chain nitrogen of a lysine residue (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1274] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 26). LTQ was first identified and characterized through the biochemical investigation of a peptide from bovine aorta lysyl oxidase (LOX), a protein responsible for the posttranslational modification of elastin and collagen in the metabolism of connective tissue."
                    - superscript [ref=e1275]:
                      - link "110" [ref=e1276] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit110"
                    - text: Reminiscent of TPQ, the formation of LTQ is also a copper and oxygen-dependent autocatalytic process, and is required for catalysis. Due to its membership in the CuAO family, it is assumed that the mechanism of formation of LTQ is similar to that of TPQ, but with the addition of the lysine nitrogen into the orthoquinone intermediate.
                    - superscript [ref=e1277]:
                      - link "4" [ref=e1278] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit4"
                    - text: A 2.4 Å resolution crystal structure of human lysyl oxidase-like 2 (hLOXL2) has a zinc ion occupying the copper-binding site, which prevented LTQ formation but the LTQ forming residues were observed 16.6 Å away from each other, suggesting that the published structure is in a precursor state to LTQ formation.
                    - superscript [ref=e1279]:
                      - link "211" [ref=e1280] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit211"
                    - text: The structure of the mature LOXL2 containing the LTQ cofactor is predicted through molecular modeling and simulations.
                    - superscript [ref=e1281]:
                      - link "212" [ref=e1282] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit212"
                    - text: However, a crystal or cryo-EM structure of LOX with a mature LTQ cofactor has yet to be determined experimentally.
                - generic [ref=e1283]:
                  - heading "3.4. Cys–Lys crosslink with a NOS bridge in transaldolase" [level=3] [ref=e1284]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1285]:
                    - text: Transaldolase, a pivotal enzyme in the pentose phosphate pathway across all domains of life, has garnered significant attention as a potential drug target. Recent studies by Tittman and colleagues on the transaldolase enzyme of
                    - emphasis [ref=e1286]: Neisseria gonnorhoeae
                    - text: (
                    - emphasis [ref=e1287]: Ng
                    - text: TAL) has revealed an unprecedented redox-regulated mechanism mediated by a unique protein-derived cofactor (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1288] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 27)."
                    - superscript [ref=e1289]:
                      - link "182" [ref=e1290] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit182"
                    - text: This discovery underscores the enzyme's potential as a therapeutic target for combating gonorrhea, a sexually transmitted disease of global concern.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1291]:
                    - text: The study demonstrates that
                    - emphasis [ref=e1292]: Ng
                    - text: TAL's enzymatic activity is modulated by redox conditions. Site-directed mutagenesis of its three cysteine residues revealed that none of these form disulfide bonds, yet the loss of redox regulation upon substitution of Cys38 pointed to its critical role. Structural characterization of the oxidized and reduced states of
                    - emphasis [ref=e1293]: Ng
                    - text: TAL provided further insights. In the reduced state,
                    - emphasis [ref=e1294]: Ng
                    - text: TAL exhibits catalytic activity, while in the oxidized state, activity is abolished. Crystallographic analyses of the oxidized form unveiled a novel covalent crosslink between Cys38 and Lys8, bridged by an additional oxygen atom—forming an NOS (nitrogen–oxygen–sulfur) bridge (
                    - link "Fig. 11B" [ref=e1295] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig11"
                    - text: ). This crosslink is absent in the reduced state (PDB 3CLM), wherein molecular oxygen is observed near Cys38, suggesting its role in initiating the oxidation that leads to the formation of the NOS bridge.
                    - superscript [ref=e1296]:
                      - link "182" [ref=e1297] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit182"
                  - figure [ref=e1298]:
                    - heading "Fig. 11. (A) General cofactor biogenesis mechanism of NOS/SONOS bridge.213 Crystal structures of NOS/SONOS redox switches. (B) Oxidized NgTAL, showing the NOS bridge formed by the crosslink of Lys8 and Cys38 (turquoise; PDB 6ZX4). (C) Mpro of SARS-CoV-2 in complex with inhibitor MPI8, possessing a SONOS bridge formed by the crosslink of Cys44, Lys61, and Cys22 (gray; PDB 7UUA)." [level=4] [ref=e1299]:
                      - text: Fig. 11. (A) General cofactor biogenesis mechanism of NOS/SONOS bridge.
                      - superscript [ref=e1300]:
                        - link "213" [ref=e1301] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit213"
                      - text: Crystal structures of NOS/SONOS redox switches. (B) Oxidized
                      - emphasis [ref=e1302]: Ng
                      - text: TAL, showing the NOS bridge formed by the crosslink of Lys8 and Cys38 (turquoise; PDB 6ZX4). (C) M
                      - superscript [ref=e1303]: pro
                      - text: of SARS-CoV-2 in complex with inhibitor MPI8, possessing a SONOS bridge formed by the crosslink of Cys44, Lys61, and Cys22 (gray; PDB 7UUA).
                    - paragraph [ref=e1304]:
                      - link "Fig. 11 🔍" [ref=e1305] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f11.jpg
                        - img "Fig. 11" [ref=e1306]
                        - text: 🔍
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1308] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/fig11/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1309]: The NOS bridge distinguishes itself from other protein-derived cofactors by its reversibility, enabling it to function as an allosteric redox switch. Under oxidizing conditions, the formation of the NOS bridge disrupts enzymatic activity, whereas reducing conditions restore the enzyme's functionality by breaking the crosslink. This reversible mechanism provides an elegant means of modulating enzyme activity in response to cellular redox states.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1310]:
                    - text: Computational studies shed light on the plausible mechanism of NOS bridge formation. The favored pathway involves the oxidation of the cysteine thiol by reactive oxygen species (ROS) to generate sulfenic acid, while the lysine amine undergoes oxidation to form hydroxylamine or an amine oxide.
                    - superscript [ref=e1311]:
                      - link "214" [ref=e1312] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit214"
                    - text: A condensation reaction between sulfenic acid and hydroxylamine, accompanied by water elimination, results in the formation of the NOS bridge. This mechanism not only highlights the versatility of oxidative modifications in enzyme regulation but also adds to the growing repertoire of protein-derived cofactors with unique functional and structural properties. Similarly, cysteinesulfenic acid, Cys-(
                    - emphasis [ref=e1313]: S
                    - text: )-OH, and derivatives stabilized by elements of protein structure as novel protein cofactors in enzyme catalysis and redox regulation have been appreciated.
                    - superscript [ref=e1314]:
                      - link "17,33–35" [ref=e1315] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit17"
                - generic [ref=e1316]:
                  - heading "3.5. Cys–Tyr crosslinks" [level=3] [ref=e1317]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1318]:
                    - text: Cys–Tyr crosslinks are versatile protein-derived cofactors that differ in their functional roles depending on their structural context. While radical-containing Cys–Tyr cofactors are directly involved in catalysis as the catalytic driving force to provide an oxidizing equivalent to the bound substrate (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1319] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 29), radical-free Cys–Tyr crosslinks ("
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1320] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 28) often serve to amplify catalytic efficiency."
                    - superscript [ref=e1321]:
                      - link "12,215" [ref=e1322] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit12"
                    - text: These crosslinks are integral to the activity of a range of enzymes, including thiol dioxygenases and reductases, underscoring their importance in diverse biochemical processes.
                  - generic [ref=e1323]:
                    - 'heading "3.5.1. Cys–Tyr in thiol dioxygenases: catalytic amplification in thiol metabolism and oxygen sensing" [level=4] [ref=e1324]'
                    - paragraph [ref=e1325]:
                      - text: Cysteine dioxygenase (CDO) and cysteamine dioxygenase (ADO) are the only known mammalian thiol dioxygenases. They utilize a mononuclear nonheme iron center to catalyze oxygen insertion into sulfur-containing substrates. CDO converts l-cysteine to cysteine sulfinic acid, playing a crucial role in thiol metabolism and redox homeostasis. Similarly, ADO catalyzes the dioxygenation of cysteamine and
                      - emphasis [ref=e1326]: "N"
                      - text: "-terminal cysteine-containing proteins, such as regulators of G-protein signaling (RGS4 and RGS5) in the"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1327]: "N"
                      - text: "-degron pathway and interleukin-32."
                      - superscript [ref=e1328]:
                        - link "216–220" [ref=e1329] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit216"
                      - text: Thus, these enzymes play critical roles in thiol metabolism and redox regulations.
                      - superscript [ref=e1330]:
                        - link "221,222" [ref=e1331] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit221"
                    - paragraph [ref=e1332]:
                      - text: The Cys–Tyr crosslink was first observed in the mouse CDO crystal structure,
                      - superscript [ref=e1333]:
                        - link "188" [ref=e1334] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit188"
                      - text: and later in rat and human CDO structures (
                      - link "Fig. 12A" [ref=e1335] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig12"
                      - text: ).
                      - superscript [ref=e1336]:
                        - link "14,223" [ref=e1337] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit14"
                      - text: This crosslink is not essential for catalysis but enhances catalytic efficiency by approximately 20-fold. Initially, Tyr157 was believed to initiate cofactor formation;
                      - superscript [ref=e1338]:
                        - link "224" [ref=e1339] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit224"
                      - text: however, recent structural and computational studies indicate that Cys93 undergoes the initial oxidation.
                      - superscript [ref=e1340]:
                        - link "14,225" [ref=e1341] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit14"
                      - text: The protein-derived cofactor is “untouchable” by traditional site-directed mutagenesis, as such mutations disable cofactor synthesis.
                    - figure [ref=e1342]:
                      - heading "Fig. 12. Cys–Tyr cofactor in mammalian thiol dioxygenases. (A) Cysteine-bound F2-Tyr157 hCDO (lime green; PDB 6BPV). (B) Cofactor-free Ni-ADO (salmon; PDB 7REI) is hexa-coordinated by three histidine residues, and three water molecules. Cofactor formation requires iron, cysteamine, and molecular oxygen therefore the crosslink is not expected in the Ni-bound crystal structure, but the crosslink has been detected via mass-spec in previous work." [level=5] [ref=e1343]:
                        - text: Fig. 12. Cys–Tyr cofactor in mammalian thiol dioxygenases. (A) Cysteine-bound F
                        - subscript [ref=e1344]: "2"
                        - text: "-Tyr157 hCDO (lime green; PDB 6BPV). (B) Cofactor-free Ni-ADO (salmon; PDB 7REI) is hexa-coordinated by three histidine residues, and three water molecules. Cofactor formation requires iron, cysteamine, and molecular oxygen therefore the crosslink is not expected in the Ni-bound crystal structure, but the crosslink has been detected"
                        - emphasis [ref=e1345]: via
                        - text: mass-spec in previous work.
                      - paragraph [ref=e1346]:
                        - link "Fig. 12 🔍" [ref=e1347] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f12.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 12" [ref=e1348]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1350] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/fig12/
                    - paragraph [ref=e1351]:
                      - text: By introducing non-proteinogenic unnatural amino acids to specifically substitute the cofactor-bearing residues through genetic code expansion, the Cys–Tyr crosslink mechanism in human CDO has become better understood.
                      - superscript [ref=e1352]:
                        - link "14,225" [ref=e1353] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit14"
                      - text: Subsequent studies in human ADO
                      - superscript [ref=e1354]:
                        - link "12" [ref=e1355] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit12"
                      - text: and galactose oxidase
                      - superscript [ref=e1356]:
                        - link "13" [ref=e1357] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit13"
                      - text: have contributed to this understanding and showcased that this non-canonical amino acid substitution is a powerful approach to studying protein-derived crosslink cofactors. The ternary complex structure of a genetically modified variant with 3,5-difluoro-l-tyrosine 157 (F
                      - subscript [ref=e1358]: "2"
                      - text: "-Tyr157) CDO bound to substrate cysteine and an O"
                      - subscript [ref=e1359]: "2"
                      - text: structural surrogate nitric oxide (˙NO) at 1.96 Å resolution revealed that ˙NO is equidistant (3.1 Å) from both Cys93 and Tyr157 residues, with F
                      - subscript [ref=e1360]: "2"
                      - text: "-Tyr157 additionally hydrogen-bonded to the substrate's carboxyl group."
                      - superscript [ref=e1361]:
                        - link "225" [ref=e1362] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit225"
                      - text: This interaction suggests that Cys93 is the preferred site of initial oxidation.
                      - superscript [ref=e1363]:
                        - link "14,225" [ref=e1364] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit14"
                    - paragraph [ref=e1365]:
                      - text: Mechanistically, substrate binding to the ferrous iron center generates an iron-bound superoxide radical, which oxidizes Cys93 to produce a thiyl radical. Tyr157 is subsequently oxidized by this thiyl radical, forming the Cys–Tyr crosslink (
                      - link "Fig. 13" [ref=e1366] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig13"
                      - text: ).
                      - superscript [ref=e1367]:
                        - link "225" [ref=e1368] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit225"
                      - text: CDO plays a central role in thiol metabolism with its substrate level fluctuating in cells in a large range depending on the metabolic states of fed, fasting, and starvation, it is proposed that the Cys–Tyr cofactor is formed after hundreds of turnovers to amplify the catalytic efficiency in the presence of excess substrate.
                      - superscript [ref=e1369]:
                        - link "226" [ref=e1370] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit226"
                      - text: This cofactor formation was first reported in 2006, establishing CDO as a model system for studying thiol dioxygenases.
                    - figure [ref=e1371]:
                      - heading "Fig. 13. Proposed mechanism of formation of Cys–Tyr crosslink in CDO.225 Upon substrate binding to the Fe(ii) center, an iron-bound superoxide radical is generated. The radical oxidizes Cys93, forming a thiyl radical and iron-bound hydroperoxide. The thiyl radical oxidizes Tyr157, generating the Cys–Tyr crosslink, and a transient-state radical species in Tyr157. C–H bond cleavage is driven by the formation of a ketone species after deprotonation of the hydroxyl group in the iron-bound hydroperoxide." [level=5] [ref=e1372]:
                        - text: Fig. 13. Proposed mechanism of formation of Cys–Tyr crosslink in CDO.
                        - superscript [ref=e1373]:
                          - link "225" [ref=e1374] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#cit225"
                        - text: Upon substrate binding to the Fe(ii) center, an iron-bound superoxide radical is generated. The radical oxidizes Cys93, forming a thiyl radical and iron-bound hydroperoxide. The thiyl radical oxidizes Tyr157, generating the Cys–Tyr crosslink, and a transient-state radical species in Tyr157. C–H bond cleavage is driven by the formation of a ketone species after deprotonation of the hydroxyl group in the iron-bound hydroperoxide.
                      - paragraph [ref=e1375]:
                        - link "Fig. 13 🔍" [ref=e1376] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f13.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 13" [ref=e1377]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1379] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/fig13/
                    - paragraph [ref=e1380]:
                      - text: In 2018, ADO was discovered to harbor a Cys–Tyr cofactor through mass spectrometry and
                      - superscript [ref=e1381]: "19"
                      - text: F-NMR spectroscopy, representing a variation of this motif (
                      - link "Fig. 12B" [ref=e1382] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#fig12"
                      - text: ).
                      - superscript [ref=e1383]:
                        - link "12" [ref=e1384] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit12"
                      - text: Unlike CDO, ADO's cofactor residues, Cys220 and Tyr222, are located adjacent to each other in the protein sequence, allowing the crosslink to form without inducing significant structural changes. This proximity confers structural flexibility, enabling ADO to accept substrates of varying sizes, from small thiol metabolites like cysteamine to larger
                      - emphasis [ref=e1385]: "N"
                      - text: "-cysteine peptides. The presence of the Cys220-Tyr222 crosslink increases the catalytic rate of ADO by more than 10-fold"
                      - superscript [ref=e1386]:
                        - link "12" [ref=e1387] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit12"
                      - text: through a concerted dioxygen transfer mechanism.
                      - superscript [ref=e1388]:
                        - link "227" [ref=e1389] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit227"
                    - paragraph [ref=e1390]:
                      - text: Importantly, ADO exhibits dual functionality in thiol metabolism and oxygen sensing, leveraging its Cys–Tyr cofactor for catalytic enhancement only in thiol metabolism. The cofactor is not needed for oxygen sensing. Since the oxygenation of large protein substrates does not require a catalytic rate boost as cysteamine does during various metabolic states, the Cys–Tyr cofactor is not always synthesized in ADO.
                      - superscript [ref=e1391]:
                        - link "217,219" [ref=e1392] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit217"
                      - text: Additionally, Cys220 and Tyr222 sit in a dynamic loop that likely moves away from the catalytic iron center when the target residue of a protein substrate arrives.
                      - superscript [ref=e1393]:
                        - link "217" [ref=e1394] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit217"
                      - text: This trait makes the Cys–Tyr crosslink less frequently encountered in ADO and challenging to observe. It has been detected by protein mass spectrometry for the wild-type enzyme and shows approximately 50% occupancy for crosslinked and uncrosslinked forms in the F
                      - subscript [ref=e1395]: "2"
                      - text: "-Tyr222 human ADO variant."
                      - superscript [ref=e1396]:
                        - link "12" [ref=e1397] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit12"
                      - text: Like LTQ,
                      - superscript [ref=e1398]:
                        - link "211" [ref=e1399] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit211"
                      - text: a crystal or cryo-EM structure of ADO with a mature Cys–Tyr cofactor remains unavailable to date, even though its uncrosslinked cofactorless structure is available for both human
                      - superscript [ref=e1400]:
                        - link "217" [ref=e1401] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit217"
                      - text: and mouse
                      - superscript [ref=e1402]:
                        - link "218" [ref=e1403] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit218"
                      - text: ADO proteins.
                  - generic [ref=e1404]:
                    - heading "3.5.2. Cys–Tyr in reductive and nitrosative stress responses" [level=4] [ref=e1405]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1406]:
                      - text: Beyond thiol dioxygenases, Cys–Tyr crosslinks have been identified in sulfite reductase, cytochrome
                      - emphasis [ref=e1407]: c
                      - text: nitrite reductase, and putative zinc protease BF4112.
                      - superscript [ref=e1408]:
                        - link "15,16" [ref=e1409] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit15"
                      - text: These enzymes exemplify the broader functional spectrum of Cys–Tyr crosslinks, from catalytic amplification to electron transfer and structural stabilization. In sulfite reductase, the Cys–Tyr crosslink stabilizes the enzyme's active site, facilitating electron transfer during the reduction of sulfite to hydrogen sulfide. Similarly, cytochrome
                      - emphasis [ref=e1410]: c
                      - text: nitrite reductase utilizes this crosslink for nitrite reduction under nitrosative stress, showcasing the adaptability of this cofactor across diverse reaction mechanisms.
                    - paragraph [ref=e1411]: The unique properties of Cys–Tyr crosslinks, particularly their ability to amplify enzymatic efficiency and stabilize catalytic intermediates, make them a recurring motif in enzyme evolution. Continued exploration of these crosslinks promises to uncover new insights into their functional roles and mechanistic diversity in biological systems.
                - generic [ref=e1412]:
                  - heading "3.6. Stable (Cys–Tyr)˙ cofactor in copper-radical oxidases" [level=3] [ref=e1413]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1414]:
                    - text: The (Cys–Tyr)˙ cofactor represents a remarkable example of a stable yet catalytically potent crosslinked radical cofactor distinct from those derived from a single amino acid such as glycyl or tyrosyl radicals in RNR and PS II. It is a copper ligand in a superfamily of copper-radical oxidases oxidizing a variety of diverse carbohydrates.
                    - superscript [ref=e1415]:
                      - link "228,229" [ref=e1416] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit228"
                    - text: Permanently stabilized within the enzyme's active site, this tyrosyl radical (Tyr˙),
                    - superscript [ref=e1417]:
                      - link "186,230" [ref=e1418] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit186"
                    - text: covalently linked to a cysteine residue
                    - emphasis [ref=e1419]: via
                    - text: a thioether bond,
                    - superscript [ref=e1420]:
                      - link "2,187" [ref=e1421] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit2"
                    - text: is akin to the tyrosyl radical in RNR. However, its reactivity is finely tuned to serve as the catalytic driving force for the specific substrates of its host enzyme. This duality of stability and reactivity distinguishes the (Cys–Tyr)˙ cofactor from the radical-free Cys–Tyr crosslinks discussed earlier.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1422]:
                    - text: In copper-dependent galactose oxidase (GAO), the (Cys–Tyr)˙ cofactor is central to the enzyme's ability to catalyze the two-electron oxidation of a wide range of primary alcohols to their corresponding aldehydes, coupled with the reduction of molecular oxygen to hydrogen peroxide.
                    - superscript [ref=e1423]:
                      - link "2,229,231–233" [ref=e1424] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit2"
                    - text: This reaction is facilitated by the synergy between the (Cys–Tyr)˙ radical and the Cu(ii) ion, a unique coupling that enables two-electron redox chemistry with a single copper center.
                    - superscript [ref=e1425]:
                      - link "229,231,233,234" [ref=e1426] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit229"
                    - text: The (Cys–Tyr)˙ radical is a metal ligand and permanently stabilized through antiferromagnetic coupling with the Cu(ii) ion, making it EPR-silent, as confirmed by Whittaker and Whittaker using X-ray absorption near-edge spectroscopy (XANES).
                    - superscript [ref=e1427]:
                      - link "230,235" [ref=e1428] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit230"
                    - text: The antiferromagnetic coupling renders both paramagnetic centers, Cu(ii), and the (Cys–Tyr)˙ ligand, spectroscopically silent in EPR experiments. However, the hidden Cu(ii) center becomes EPR-active when the (Cys–Tyr)˙ radical is neutralized by a radical scavenger, hydroxyurea, and hidden again upon one-electron oxidation by K
                    - subscript [ref=e1429]: "3"
                    - text: Fe(CN)
                    - subscript [ref=e1430]: "6"
                    - text: .
                    - superscript [ref=e1431]:
                      - link "13" [ref=e1432] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit13"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1433]:
                    - text: The radical cofactor is formed by the covalent linkage of Cys228 and Tyr272 through an autocatalytic, irreversible posttranslational modification.
                    - superscript [ref=e1434]:
                      - link "229,231" [ref=e1435] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit229"
                    - text: The crystal structure of GAO revealed that the sulfur atom of Cys228 forms a thioether bond with the
                    - emphasis [ref=e1436]: C
                    - subscript [ref=e1437]: ε
                    - text: of Tyr272, a crosslink essential for catalytic function.
                    - superscript [ref=e1438]:
                      - link "187" [ref=e1439] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit187"
                    - text: This configuration supports the radical's stability while allowing it to participate in substrate oxidation.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1440]:
                    - text: Mutagenesis studies have reinforced the importance of the cofactor. Substitution of Cys228 with glycine (C228G) abolished cofactor formation and reduced catalytic efficiency by approximately 1000-fold, underscoring the critical role of the sulfur atom in stabilizing the Tyr˙ radical.
                    - superscript [ref=e1441]:
                      - link "236,237" [ref=e1442] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit236"
                    - text: Synthetic model studies have provided additional mechanistic insights, demonstrating that thioether substitutions lower the phenol's p
                    - emphasis [ref=e1443]: K
                    - subscript [ref=e1444]: a
                    - text: and reduction potential, facilitating radical formation and stabilization.
                    - superscript [ref=e1445]:
                      - link "234" [ref=e1446] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit234"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1447]:
                    - text: Recent advances in genetic code expansion have offered new perspectives on the cofactor's properties and formation.
                    - superscript [ref=e1448]:
                      - link "238" [ref=e1449] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit238"
                    - text: Substitution of Tyr272 with F
                    - subscript [ref=e1450]: "2"
                    - text: "-Tyr retained cofactor formation but reduced catalytic efficiency to 12% of the wild-type enzyme. Structural studies of F"
                    - subscript [ref=e1451]: "2"
                    - text: "-Tyr GAO revealed the formation of a monofluorinated radical cofactor"
                    - emphasis [ref=e1452]: via
                    - text: C–F bond cleavage, illustrating how electronic modifications influence the radical's reactivity and stability (
                    - link "Fig. 14" [ref=e1453] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig14"
                    - text: ).
                    - superscript [ref=e1454]:
                      - link "13,239" [ref=e1455] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit13"
                  - figure [ref=e1456]:
                    - heading "Fig. 14. Active sites of GAO and F2-Tyr272 GAO showing the (Cys–Tyr)˙ cofactor. (A) Active site of GAO (purple; PDB 6XLT) shows the crosslink between Tyr272 and Cys228, with the copper ion coordinated in square pyramidal geometry by two histidines, Tyr495, and cofactor-bearing Tyr272. (B) Active site of F2-Tyr272 GAO (teal; PDB 6XLS) retaining a monofluorinated (Cys–Tyr)˙ cofactor through the oxidative C–F bond cleavage during autocatalytic cofactor formation.13." [level=4] [ref=e1457]:
                      - text: Fig. 14. Active sites of GAO and F
                      - subscript [ref=e1458]: "2"
                      - text: "-Tyr272 GAO showing the (Cys–Tyr)˙ cofactor. (A) Active site of GAO (purple; PDB 6XLT) shows the crosslink between Tyr272 and Cys228, with the copper ion coordinated in square pyramidal geometry by two histidines, Tyr495, and cofactor-bearing Tyr272. (B) Active site of F"
                      - subscript [ref=e1459]: "2"
                      - text: "-Tyr272 GAO (teal; PDB 6XLS) retaining a monofluorinated (Cys–Tyr)˙ cofactor through the oxidative C–F bond cleavage during autocatalytic cofactor formation."
                      - superscript [ref=e1460]:
                        - link "13" [ref=e1461] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit13"
                      - text: .
                    - paragraph [ref=e1462]:
                      - link "Fig. 14 🔍" [ref=e1463] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f14.jpg
                        - img "Fig. 14" [ref=e1464]
                        - text: 🔍
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1466] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/fig14/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1467]:
                    - text: The proposed cofactor maturation mechanism involves a copper-bound superoxide radical oxidizing the thiol group of Cys228, forming a copper-bound hydroperoxide intermediate and a thiyl radical. This thiyl radical then oxidizes Tyr272 (or F
                    - subscript [ref=e1468]: "2"
                    - text: "-Tyr272), resulting in the formation of the thioether bond and the mature (Cys–Tyr)˙ cofactor ("
                    - link "Fig. 15" [ref=e1469] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig15"
                    - text: ).
                    - superscript [ref=e1470]:
                      - link "13,239" [ref=e1471] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit13"
                  - figure [ref=e1472]:
                    - heading "Fig. 15. Proposed biogenesis mechanism of the (Cys–Tyr)˙ in F2-Tyr272 GAO." [level=4] [ref=e1473]:
                      - text: Fig. 15. Proposed biogenesis mechanism of the (Cys–Tyr)˙ in F
                      - subscript [ref=e1474]: "2"
                      - text: "-Tyr272 GAO."
                    - paragraph [ref=e1475]:
                      - link "Fig. 15 🔍" [ref=e1476] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f15.jpg
                        - img "Fig. 15" [ref=e1477]
                        - text: 🔍
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1479] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/fig15/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1480]:
                    - text: In addition to GAO, the (Cys–Tyr)˙ cofactor is found in glyoxal oxidase (GLOX) and other copper radical oxidases, where it drives diverse catalytic reactions tailored to their specific substrates.
                    - superscript [ref=e1481]:
                      - link "228,229,231" [ref=e1482] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit228"
                    - text: Its evolutionary significance and versatility underscore its importance as a powerful and highly specialized catalytic tool in enzymatic chemistry.
              - generic [ref=e1483]:
                - heading "4. Protein cofactors derived from covalent crosslink of three or more amino acids" [level=2] [ref=e1484]: 4. Protein cofactors derived from covalent crosslink of three or more amino acids
                - paragraph [ref=e1485]:
                  - text: "This section highlights protein-derived cofactors formed by the covalent crosslinking of three or more distinct amino acid residues. These trimeric or more crosslinks represent some of the most complex and functionally diverse modifications found in nature. By enhancing catalytic efficiency, stabilizing active sites, and introducing novel functionalities, these cofactors play critical roles in the biochemical processes of their host proteins. To date, four distinct trimeric crosslinked cofactors have been identified: Cys–Lys–Cys, Gly–Ala–Ser, Met–Tyr–Trp, and Ser–Tyr–Gly ("
                  - link "Table 3" [ref=e1486] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tab3"
                  - text: ). Additionally, the redox PQQ cofactor (
                  - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1487] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig1"
                  - text: ", 37) is formed from five amino acid residues Glu–Val–Thr–Leu–Tyr with further posttranslational oxidations. Below, we examine their structure, function, and synthesis mechanisms."
                - generic [ref=e1488]:
                  - heading "Table 3. Cofactors derived from covalent crosslink of three or more amino acids." [level=3] [ref=e1489]
                  - table [ref=e1491]:
                    - rowgroup [ref=e1500]:
                      - row "Source Cofactor New chemical bonds formed Representative enzyme Biogenesis Function Ref." [ref=e1501]:
                        - columnheader "Source" [ref=e1502]
                        - columnheader "Cofactor" [ref=e1503]
                        - columnheader "New chemical bonds formed" [ref=e1504]
                        - columnheader "Representative enzyme" [ref=e1505]
                        - columnheader "Biogenesis" [ref=e1506]
                        - columnheader "Function" [ref=e1507]
                        - columnheader "Ref." [ref=e1508]
                    - rowgroup [ref=e1509]:

                      - 'row "Glu–Val–Thr–Leu–Tyr PQQ C–C Alcohol dehydrogenase, aminoadipic 6-semialdehyde dehydrogenase Enzymatic: PqqE, PqqD, PqqF, PqqB, and PqqC Catalysis 203–205" [ref=e1575]':

                      - row "C–N" [ref=e1584]:
                        - cell "C–N" [ref=e1585]
                      - row "CDOUBLE BOND, LENGTH AS M-DASHO" [ref=e1586]:
                        - cell "CDOUBLE BOND, LENGTH AS M-DASHO" [ref=e1587]:
