                          - text: O
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1593] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: table/tab3/
                - generic [ref=e1594]:
                  - heading "4.1. Cys–Lys–Cys crosslink with a SONOS bridge" [level=3] [ref=e1595]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1596]:
                    - text: The Cys–Lys–Cys cofactor represents a fascinating chemical feature involving a sulfur–oxygen–nitrogen–oxygen–sulfur (SONOS) bridge (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1597] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 33). Initially identified in the main protease (M"
                    - superscript [ref=e1598]: pro
                    - text: ) of SARS-CoV-2, this structure has garnered significant attention due to its role in the pathogenesis and replication of the virus, making it a prime drug target in COVID-19 research.
                    - superscript [ref=e1599]:
                      - link "213,240" [ref=e1600] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit213"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1601]:
                    - text: Crystal soaking experiments revealed Y-shaped crosslinks between Cys22, Cys44, and Lys61 in M
                    - superscript [ref=e1602]: pro
                    - text: (
                    - link "Fig. 11C" [ref=e1603] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig11"
                    - text: ). Computational studies suggest that the SONOS bridge forms through a series of oxidation reactions, likely mediated by reactive oxygen species (ROS).
                    - superscript [ref=e1604]:
                      - link "214" [ref=e1605] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit214"
                    - text: A thio-(hydro)peroxy acid intermediate has been identified as a plausible precursor to both NOS and SONOS bridges, with homolytic dissociation leading to NOS formation. However, the exact pathway for SONOS formation remains under investigation.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1606]:
                    - text: The regulatory role of SONOS and NOS bridges in M
                    - superscript [ref=e1607]: pro
                    - text: exemplifies their functional versatility. The reversible formation and dissolution of these crosslinks appear to act as redox switches, modulating enzymatic activity in response to oxidative stress. Further structural and mechanistic studies are crucial for understanding the formation and potential therapeutic exploitation of these cofactors.
                - generic [ref=e1608]:
                  - heading "4.2. Ser–Tyr–Gly crosslink in green fluorescent protein" [level=3] [ref=e1609]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1610]:
                    - text: The Ser–Tyr–Gly crosslink forms the fluorescent chromophore at the core of green fluorescent protein (GFP), a widely used tool in molecular and cellular biology.
                    - superscript [ref=e1611]:
                      - link "245" [ref=e1612] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit245"
                    - text: GFP's chromophore is generated by the autocatalytic cyclization and oxidation of the Ser65 (or Thr65), Tyr66, and Gly67 residues within a tightly folded β-barrel structure (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1613] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 34)."
                  - paragraph [ref=e1614]:
                    - text: Unlike many cofactors, the formation of GFP's chromophore is spontaneous and requires no external enzymes. However, molecular oxygen is essential, as is precise protein folding to orient the residues for cyclization. The resultant chromophore exhibits a unique conjugated structure that absorbs blue light and emits green fluorescence (
                    - link "Fig. 16" [ref=e1615] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig16"
                    - text: ).
                  - figure [ref=e1616]:
                    - heading "Fig. 16. Crystal structure of GFP cofactor formed by the cyclization and oxidation of Thr65, Tyr66, and Gly67 (pink; PDB 1EMA)." [level=4] [ref=e1617]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1618]:
                      - link "Fig. 16 🔍" [ref=e1619] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f16.jpg
                        - img "Fig. 16" [ref=e1620]
                        - text: 🔍
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1622] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/fig16/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1623]:
                    - text: Mechanistic parallels have been drawn between the GFP chromophore and the methylene imidazolone (MIO) cofactor in histidase enzymes,
                    - superscript [ref=e1624]:
                      - link "246" [ref=e1625] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit246"
                    - text: suggesting a shared evolutionary origin (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1626] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 35)."
                    - superscript [ref=e1627]:
                      - link "21" [ref=e1628] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit21"
                    - text: Despite extensive use, the detailed mechanism of GFP chromophore biosynthesis remains elusive. Ongoing studies aim to elucidate these pathways, which may enable the engineering of fluorescent proteins with novel properties.
                - generic [ref=e1629]:
                  - heading "4.3. Met+–Tyr–Trp cofactor in catalase-peroxidase" [level=3] [ref=e1630]:
                    - text: 4.3. Met
                    - superscript [ref=e1631]: +
                    - text: –Tyr–Trp cofactor in catalase-peroxidase
                  - paragraph [ref=e1632]:
                    - text: In the catalase-peroxidase enzyme (KatG), the Met
                    - superscript [ref=e1633]: +
                    - text: –Tyr–Trp (MYW) cofactor is a trimeric crosslink critical for its bifunctional activity. KatG is a well-known enzyme, largely due to
                    - emphasis [ref=e1634]: Mycobacterium tuberculosis
                    - text: KatG is an essential utility for activating the frontline antitubercular prodrug isoniazid (INH) and detoxifying reactive oxygen species such as H
                    - subscript [ref=e1635]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1636]: "2"
                    - text: ", contributing to the pathogen's survival and virulence."
                    - superscript [ref=e1637]:
                      - link "247" [ref=e1638] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit247"
                    - text: This bifunctional enzyme has emerged as a promising target in tuberculosis drug development, with chemical modification of the MYW cofactor rendering the bacterium more susceptible to peroxide-mediated immune clearance and simultaneously enhancing peroxidase activity to improve INH activation.
                    - superscript [ref=e1639]:
                      - link "248" [ref=e1640] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit248"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1641]:
                    - text: The MYW cofactor, first characterized in
                    - emphasis [ref=e1642]: Haloarcula marismortui
                    - text: KatG by X-crystallography, is autocatalytically formed during protein maturation.
                    - superscript [ref=e1643]:
                      - link "249" [ref=e1644] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit249"
                    - text: Spectroscopic studies suggest that an Fe(iv)
                    - generic "DOUBLE BOND, LENGTH AS M-DASH" [ref=e1645]:
                      - img [ref=e1646]
                    - text: O porphyrin cation radical oxidizes Met, Tyr, and Trp residues to generate the crosslinked structure.
                    - superscript [ref=e1649]:
                      - link "250,251" [ref=e1650] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit250"
                    - text: This cofactor endows KatG with an exceptional single-function catalase-like activity, synergistically enhancing H
                    - subscript [ref=e1651]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1652]: "2"
                    - text: detoxification by several orders of magnitude while retaining its innate peroxidase functionality.
                    - superscript [ref=e1653]:
                      - link "251,252" [ref=e1654] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit251"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1655]:
                    - text: It has been proven through EPR coupled with isotope labeling that MYW becomes a transient MYW˙ free radical after H
                    - subscript [ref=e1656]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1657]: "2"
                    - text: binds to the adjacent heme and becomes activated, and a cofactor radical-based catalase mechanism is thus proposed.
                    - superscript [ref=e1658]:
                      - link "253" [ref=e1659] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit253"
                    - text: The MYW cofactor spares one of the oxidizing equivalents and located next to the Fe(iv)
                    - generic "DOUBLE BOND, LENGTH AS M-DASH" [ref=e1660]:
                      - img [ref=e1661]
                    - text: O, enabling strong coupling for 2e
                    - superscript [ref=e1664]: −
                    - text: oxidation chemistry against subsequent H
                    - subscript [ref=e1665]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1666]: "2"
                    - text: for catalase activity. Otherwise, the oxidizing equivalents from H
                    - subscript [ref=e1667]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1668]: "2"
                    - text: are directed outside the heme center of KatG
                    - emphasis [ref=e1669]: via
                    - text: aromatic residues to support 1e
                    - superscript [ref=e1670]: −
                    - text: oxidation of organic substrates (
                    - emphasis [ref=e1671]: i.e.
                    - text: ", peroxidase activity)."
                    - superscript [ref=e1672]:
                      - link "252" [ref=e1673] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit252"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1674]:
                    - text: "Interestingly, two forms of the MYW cofactor exist in nature: MYW (catalase-active) and MYW-OOH (catalase-inhibitory) ("
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1675] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 36a and 36b)."
                    - superscript [ref=e1676]:
                      - link "254–256" [ref=e1677] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit254"
                    - text: Studies of the solution state of as-isolated
                    - emphasis [ref=e1678]: M. tuberculosis
                    - text: KatG indicate that the MYW-OOH cofactor can act as a molecular switch, toggling between active MYW and inactive MYW-OOH states depending on its environment conditions for the chemical structure of the trimeric cofactor, such as temperature and hydrogen peroxide levels.
                    - superscript [ref=e1679]:
                      - link "254" [ref=e1680] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit254"
                    - text: This dual functionality underscores the evolutionary significance of the MYW cofactor in balancing oxidative defense and metabolic processes.
                - generic [ref=e1681]:
                  - heading "4.4. Pyrroloquinoline quinone (PQQ) cofactor in dehydrogenases" [level=3] [ref=e1682]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1683]:
                    - text: The crosslinked protein cofactor PQQ (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1684] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", 37) found in alcohol dehydrogenases and aminoadipic 6-semialdehyde dehydrogenase,"
                    - superscript [ref=e1685]:
                      - link "203–205" [ref=e1686] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit203"
                    - text: possesses two remarkably unique features. First, it is the only known protein cofactor derived from more than three amino acid residues. Second, it is the only one that does not remain covalently bound to the parent protein. The synthesis of PQQ starts from a tyrosine residue and subsequently involves crosslinking and further chemical modifications of glutamate, valine, threonine, and leucine residues (
                    - link "Fig. 17" [ref=e1687] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig17"
                    - text: ).
                    - superscript [ref=e1688]:
                      - link "67,257" [ref=e1689] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit67"
                    - text: "This complex process is facilitated by a series of auxiliary proteins: PqqE, PqqD, PqqF, PqqB, and PqqC. During the post-translational modifications (PTMs), this quinone cofactor loses the peptide connection but remains bound at the active site of the protein."
                    - superscript [ref=e1690]:
                      - link "203" [ref=e1691] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit203"
                  - figure [ref=e1692]:
                    - heading "Fig. 17. Enzymatic synthesis PQQ consists of nine steps including crosslink and chemical modifications of five amino acid residues.67." [level=4] [ref=e1693]:
                      - text: Fig. 17. Enzymatic synthesis PQQ consists of nine steps including crosslink and chemical modifications of five amino acid residues.
                      - superscript [ref=e1694]:
                        - link "67" [ref=e1695] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#cit67"
                      - text: .
                    - paragraph [ref=e1696]:
                      - link "Fig. 17 🔍" [ref=e1697] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f17.jpg
                        - img "Fig. 17" [ref=e1698]
                        - text: 🔍
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1700] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/fig17/
              - generic [ref=e1701]:
                - heading "5. Covalent crosslinks between heme and protein residues in metalloenzymes" [level=2] [ref=e1702]: 5. Covalent crosslinks between heme and protein residues in metalloenzymes
                - paragraph [ref=e1703]:
                  - text: Covalent linkages between heme and protein residues play crucial roles in the function and stability of heme-dependent enzymes. One well-known example is heme
                  - emphasis [ref=e1704]: c
                  - text: ", which is always covalently attached to two cysteine (Cys) residues of the associated protein. Its attachment occurs through thioether bonds formed between the vinyl side chains of the heme and the cysteine residues in the protein, creating a protein cofactor with the structure Cys–heme–Cys, which could be #38 if it were included in"
                  - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1705] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig1"
                  - text: . While electron transfer is the primary function, heme
                  - emphasis [ref=e1706]: c
                  - text: also plays other roles, such as in apoptosis and serving as a catalytic site in some enzymes. Due to its prevalence, heme
                  - emphasis [ref=e1707]: c
                  - text: is not typically included in the protein-derived cofactor category.
                - paragraph [ref=e1708]:
                  - text: These crosslinks, formed between heme substituents (such as vinyl groups) and amino acid side chains (like cysteine or histidine), can significantly impact catalysis and heme retention. In addition to heme
                  - emphasis [ref=e1709]: c
                  - text: ", a recent discovery shows that SfmD, a monooxygenase involved in saframycin A biosynthesis, contains a mono-covalently linked Cys–Heme."
                  - superscript [ref=e1710]:
                    - link "36" [ref=e1711] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit36"
                  - text: SfmD possesses an autocatalytically generated thioether crosslink between the heme 4-vinyl group and Cys317 (
                  - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1712] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig1"
                  - text: ", 17). This covalent bond is essential for catalytic activity, as demonstrated by the complete loss of activity upon mutation of Cys317 to serine or alanine. The observation of Cys–heme in the crystal structure of an oxygenase in SfmD is surprising, as all other characterized members of the same superfamily, known as heme-dependent aromatic oxygenase (HDAO), contain a heme"
                  - emphasis [ref=e1713]: b
                  - text: without crosslink and thioether bond attachment.
                  - superscript [ref=e1714]:
                    - link "258" [ref=e1715] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit258"
                  - text: This unique linkage enables dynamic rotation of the heme plane during substrate binding while maintaining heme retention at the active site. Such substantial heme center dynamics are warranted in this enzyme, as the heme has two axial histidine ligands; one of these dissociates during catalysis upon substrate binding and then re-coordinates to the heme iron at the end of the catalytic cycle (
                  - link "Fig. 18A" [ref=e1716] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig18"
                  - text: ). This dynamic coordination adds a significant trigger for enhancing the oxidation power of the heme. Given that this enzyme can also utilize l-Tyr as an alternative substrate, albeit with lower efficiency compared to its native substrate, 3-methyl-l-Tyr, this unusual heme cofactor with a single covalent crosslink to a cysteine residue and an additional mobile distal histidine ligand may be crucial for preventing misfire and l-Tyr depletion.
                - figure [ref=e1717]:
                  - heading "Fig. 18. Crosslinks to heme moieties in SfmD and GlbN. (A) SfmD has a unique Cys–heme crosslink at the 4-vinyl group used for catalysis, two conformations are shown. Initially, there is bis-His coordination and presence of the crosslink (left, navy blue; PDB 6VDQ), upon addition of 3-Me-l-Tyr, ascorbate, and oxygen, His274 disassociates (right, navy blue; PDB 6VE0).36 (B) GlbN has a His–heme crosslink at the 2-vinyl group also used for catalysis. Like SfmD, it presents bis-His coordination (left, green; PDB 4MAX).259 The right side shows the proposed biogenesis mechanism of the His–heme crosslink in GlbN where the 2-vinyl group is protonated at the Cβ position to yield a Cα carbocation. The carbocation then undergoes a nucleophilic attack by His117, producing the mature crosslink.260." [level=3] [ref=e1718]:
                    - text: Fig. 18. Crosslinks to heme moieties in SfmD and GlbN. (A) SfmD has a unique Cys–heme crosslink at the 4-vinyl group used for catalysis, two conformations are shown. Initially, there is
                    - emphasis [ref=e1719]: bis
                    - text: "-His coordination and presence of the crosslink (left, navy blue; PDB 6VDQ), upon addition of 3-Me-l-Tyr, ascorbate, and oxygen, His274 disassociates (right, navy blue; PDB 6VE0)."
                    - superscript [ref=e1720]:
                      - link "36" [ref=e1721] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit36"
                    - text: (B) GlbN has a His–heme crosslink at the 2-vinyl group also used for catalysis. Like SfmD, it presents
                    - emphasis [ref=e1722]: bis
                    - text: "-His coordination (left, green; PDB 4MAX)."
                    - superscript [ref=e1723]:
                      - link "259" [ref=e1724] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit259"
                    - text: The right side shows the proposed biogenesis mechanism of the His–heme crosslink in GlbN where the 2-vinyl group is protonated at the C
                    - subscript [ref=e1725]: β
                    - text: position to yield a C
                    - subscript [ref=e1726]: α
                    - text: carbocation. The carbocation then undergoes a nucleophilic attack by His117, producing the mature crosslink.
                    - superscript [ref=e1727]:
                      - link "260" [ref=e1728] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit260"
                    - text: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e1729]:
                    - link "Fig. 18 🔍" [ref=e1730] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f18.jpg
                      - img "Fig. 18" [ref=e1731]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1733] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/fig18/
                - paragraph [ref=e1734]:
                  - text: The formation of this Cys–heme crosslink has been partially replicated in sperm whale myoglobin by introducing a cysteine residue near the heme 4-vinyl group under reducing conditions, further validating the feasibility of this type of modification.
                  - superscript [ref=e1735]:
                    - link "261" [ref=e1736] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit261"
                  - text: Another distinct type of covalent heme-protein crosslink involves a histidine residue and the heme group. This unprecedented histidine-heme bond was discovered in hemoglobins from the cyanobacteria
                  - emphasis [ref=e1737]: Synechocystis
                  - text: sp. PCC 6803 and
                  - emphasis [ref=e1738]: Synechococcus
                  - text: sp. PCC 7002 (GlbN). In these proteins, the crosslink forms between the heme 2-vinyl group and a histidine residue (
                  - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1739] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig1"
                  - text: ", 18). Site-directed mutagenesis and NMR studies have confirmed the functional role of this crosslink, particularly in catalysis."
                  - superscript [ref=e1740]:
                    - link "44,260" [ref=e1741] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit44"
                  - text: This discovery has spurred research into exploiting this type of linkage for protein engineering. Introducing a histidine residue near a heme vinyl group has been shown to improve heme retention. This strategy has been successfully applied to myoglobin and
                  - emphasis [ref=e1742]: Chlamydomonas
                  - text: hemoglobin by mimicking the crosslink observed in cyanobacteria.
                  - superscript [ref=e1743]:
                    - link "262,263" [ref=e1744] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit262"
                  - text: Reminiscent of SfmD, GlbN also has a
                  - emphasis [ref=e1745]: bis
                  - text: "-His coordination with the heme and shows oxygenation activity. GlbN is capable of ˙NO dioxygenase activity, where the His–heme crosslink enables ˙NO reduction to HNO."
                  - superscript [ref=e1746]:
                    - link "264" [ref=e1747] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit264"
                  - text: The proposed biogenesis mechanism of this modification involves the nucleophilic attack of a neutral histidine residue to a C
                  - subscript [ref=e1748]: α
                  - text: carbocation (
                  - link "Fig. 18B" [ref=e1749] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig18"
                  - text: ).
                  - superscript [ref=e1750]:
                    - link "260" [ref=e1751] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit260"
              - generic [ref=e1752]:
                - heading "6. Modern approaches revitalize crosslinked cofactor studies" [level=2] [ref=e1753]: 6. Modern approaches revitalize crosslinked cofactor studies
                - paragraph [ref=e1754]: While the discovery of protein-derived crosslinked cofactors initially sparked significant interest, the field has experienced limited progress in the past two decades. The inherent, irreversible nature of these crosslinks, forming only once per protein molecule, renders traditional mutagenesis approaches ineffective. This “untouchable” characteristic, coupled with the frequent absence of spectroscopic signatures, has hindered studies of their biogenesis and catalytic roles. Relying solely on mass spectrometry or structural determination for crosslink detection is impractical for routine analyses. Moreover, the stochastic, post-catalytic formation of these cofactors, occurring after an undefined number of catalytic cycles, further complicates mechanistic investigations. Consequently, the field has stagnated, demanding new strategies to unlock the secrets of crosslinked cofactor synthesis and function.
                - paragraph [ref=e1755]:
                  - text: Recently, an innovative approach employing site-specific non-canonical amino acid (ncAA) substitution has emerged as a powerful tool for reviving the crosslink cofactor field.
                  - superscript [ref=e1756]:
                    - link "12,14" [ref=e1757] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit12"
                  - text: By replacing cofactor-bearing residues with ncAAs, such as unnatural amino acid 3,5-difluoro-l-tyrosine, or l-3,4-dihydroxyphenylalanine (l-DOPA) from over 500 naturally occurring amino acids, the studies can maintain the essential chemical character of the target while introducing specific functional modifications. This method bypasses the limitations imposed by the irreversible crosslinks.
                - paragraph [ref=e1758]:
                  - text: "The ncAA-incorporation and site-specific cofactor substitution is an innovative, viable and rigorous approach to gain new insights into cofactor assembly and the catalytic properties of an enzyme. The ncAA incorporation strategy utilizes engineered tRNA/aminoacyl-tRNA-synthetase (aaRS) pairs and amber codon suppression to introduce desired amino acids during translation. The development of robust pEVOL vectors and commercially available ncAA systems has significantly enhanced this technology. This method consists of: (1) a plasmid that expresses aaRS pair which has been evolved to incorporate a specific ncAA, and (2) a plasmid of the gene of interest with a modified codon at the desired site (usually substituted with an amber stop codon) that will be recognized by the cognate charged tRNA ("
                  - link "Fig. 19" [ref=e1759] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig19"
                  - text: ). This method allows for the ncAA to be incorporated using native protein translation machinery. After years of resilient research, the pEVOL vector was developed to enhance non-canonical amino acid incorporation in
                  - emphasis [ref=e1760]: E. coli
                  - text: ", with at least 34 plasmids coding for different ncAAs commercially available."
                  - superscript [ref=e1761]:
                    - link "265" [ref=e1762] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit265"
                - figure [ref=e1763]:
                  - heading "Fig. 19. Cartoon diagram of genetic incorporation of non-canonical amino acids into proteins." [level=3] [ref=e1764]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1765]:
                    - link "Fig. 19 🔍" [ref=e1766] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f19.jpg
                      - img "Fig. 19" [ref=e1767]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1769] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/fig19/
                - paragraph [ref=e1770]:
                  - text: Protein-derived cofactor studies greatly benefit from the ongoing development of aaRS/tRNA
                  - subscript [ref=e1771]: CUA
                  - text: pairs that expand the toolbox to accurately and precisely probe mechanisms of formation, kinetics, and structural changes. Applications in bacterial RNR,
                  - superscript [ref=e1772]:
                    - link "266,267" [ref=e1773] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit266"
                  - text: thiol dioxygenases (CDO/ADO),
                  - superscript [ref=e1774]:
                    - link "12,14,225" [ref=e1775] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit12"
                  - text: and oxidases (GAO).
                  - superscript [ref=e1776]:
                    - link "13,238" [ref=e1777] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit13"
                  - text: These studies provided experimental evidence to support a new Cys–Tyr crosslink in ADO, direct evidence for the involvement of two tyrosine residues in radical propagation in RNR I, and evidence to support a concerted O–O transfer instead of a stepwise O-atom transfer proposed for CDO catalytic pathway,
                  - emphasis [ref=e1778]: etc.
                - paragraph [ref=e1779]:
                  - text: Continued development and expansion of ncAA incorporation techniques will be crucial for advancing our understanding of protein-derived cofactors. This approach offers a promising avenue for capturing catalytic intermediates, enabling novel reactions, and enhancing existing functions in a broad range of biological systems beyond those harboring crosslinked cofactors.
                  - superscript [ref=e1780]:
                    - link "268" [ref=e1781] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit268"
              - generic [ref=e1782]:
                - heading "7. Bridging synthetic applications and mechanistic understanding" [level=2] [ref=e1783]: 7. Bridging synthetic applications and mechanistic understanding
                - paragraph [ref=e1784]:
                  - text: "Many of the protein-derived cofactors discussed are essential for catalytic activity, either directly or indirectly. This opens two major avenues of discovery: (1) synthesizing mechanistic probes to challenge a system and gain a mechanistic understanding of the cofactors for their role in catalysis, and (2) synthesizing inhibitors to prevent the formation of essential cofactors in undesired systems. Moreover, using synthetic models, either directly or through incorporation with proteins, has garnered attention, such as in the efforts led by Hayashi and colleagues."
                  - superscript [ref=e1785]:
                    - link "79,120,269–272" [ref=e1786] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit79"
                - generic [ref=e1787]:
                  - heading "7.1. Development of biomimetic systems" [level=3] [ref=e1788]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1789]:
                    - text: Quinones have applications in organic synthesis, catalysis, and industry due to their unique redox properties. They readily undergo reversible two-electron reductions, and their ability to present multiple oxidation states (quinone, semiquinone, and hydroquinone) allows for versatile reaction outcomes.
                    - superscript [ref=e1790]:
                      - link "273" [ref=e1791] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit273"
                    - text: Quinones resembling those present in the cofactors of CuAOs and other quinoproteins have been generated to investigate quinone-catalyzed oxidations of organic substrates and compare them to quinones with high reduction potentials.
                    - superscript [ref=e1792]:
                      - link "274" [ref=e1793] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit274"
                    - text: Using PQQ as a model, phenanthroline-derived quinones were synthesized and challenged with primary and secondary amines. The reaction products supported the hypothesis of a transamination mechanism.
                    - superscript [ref=e1794]:
                      - link "275" [ref=e1795] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit275"
                    - text: This mechanistic study focused on exploring how quinone moieties are reduced by their substrates. The quinones available in biological systems that operate at ambient conditions serve as a window to investigate how other naturally occurring quinones catalyze their designated reactions. These quinones are commonly seen in fungicides, allelochemicals, and siderophores.
                    - superscript [ref=e1796]:
                      - link "273" [ref=e1797] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit273"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1798]:
                    - text: Functional models of the SCS Ni(ii) pincer complex found in LarA, also called the NPN cofactor, have been generated to study the cofactor's role in the catalytic mechanism.
                    - superscript [ref=e1799]:
                      - link "276–278" [ref=e1800] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit276"
                    - text: One of the previous models for the NPN cofactor contained thioamide groups at the C2 and C3 positions, and catalyzed alcohol dehydrogenation irreversibly with no racemization, opposite of the canonic mechanism of LarA.
                    - superscript [ref=e1801]:
                      - link "276" [ref=e1802] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit276"
                    - text: A further optimized model which replaced the thioamide groups with thiocarboxylates is more structurally similar to the NPN cofactor and displayed lactate racemization, serving as a better-suited mechanistic probe.
                    - superscript [ref=e1803]:
                      - link "277" [ref=e1804] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit277"
                    - text: Combined computational and experimental studies highlight two possible pathways, but the more favorable one involves the reversible proton-coupled hydride transfer to C1 of the NPN cofactor.
                    - superscript [ref=e1805]:
                      - link "277" [ref=e1806] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit277"
                    - text: These studies demonstrate the importance of using protein-derived cofactors as model systems when designing new metal-based catalysts and/or pincer ligands, in this case, for alcohol dehydrogenation reactions.
                    - superscript [ref=e1807]:
                      - link "276" [ref=e1808] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit276"
                  - paragraph [ref=e1809]:
                    - text: GAO is a well-rounded enzyme that has gained a lot of attention due to its ability for C–H and, recently found, C–F bond functionalization under ambient conditions.
                    - superscript [ref=e1810]:
                      - link "13" [ref=e1811] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit13"
                    - text: A substantial amount of work has been done to generate GAO model compounds that are capable of C–sp
                    - superscript [ref=e1812]: "3"
                    - text: –H oxidations with equal or greater activity/selectivity.
                    - superscript [ref=e1813]:
                      - link "234,279–283" [ref=e1814] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit234"
                    - text: Many of these GAO biomimetic systems attempt to mimic the enzyme active site or the tyrosyl radical to catalyze the same chemistry. These systems are also incorporated into the use of biosensors given that galactose quantification is highly relevant in the dairy/fermentation industries. Methods using GAO as a biosensor date back to 1977,
                    - superscript [ref=e1815]:
                      - link "284" [ref=e1816] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit284"
                    - text: and continue to be optimized in the present day.
                    - superscript [ref=e1817]:
                      - link "285" [ref=e1818] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit285"
                - generic [ref=e1819]:
                  - heading "7.2. Biocatalysis" [level=3] [ref=e1820]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1821]:
                    - text: Protein-derived cofactors have expanded the toolbox for enzymatic reactions, offering unique catalytic properties such as regio- and stereoselectivity. A prominent example is found in alcohol oxidation/dehydrogenation reactions. Copper-radical oxidases (CROs) such as galactose oxidase and glyoxal oxidases are a subset of enzymes that catalyze the two-electron oxidation of primary alcohols, concomitant with the reduction of molecular oxygen to hydrogen peroxide, without using external organic cofactors since they have a built-in Cys–Tyr radical cofactor that aids in catalysis.
                    - superscript [ref=e1822]:
                      - link "228" [ref=e1823] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit228"
                    - text: On the contrary, alcohol dehydrogenases (ADHs) and alcohol oxidases (AOXs) both require external cofactors that are either NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e1824]: +
                    - text: "-based or FAD-based for catalysis."
                    - superscript [ref=e1825]:
                      - link "286" [ref=e1826] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit286"
                    - text: The broad substrate range accepted by CROs and their ability to only require molecular oxygen for catalysis has highlighted their potential applications as environmentally friendly biocatalysts for industrial, bioengineering, and biotechnological purposes.
                    - superscript [ref=e1827]:
                      - link "287,288" [ref=e1828] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit287"
              - generic [ref=e1829]:
                - heading "8. Structural variations and function diversity" [level=2] [ref=e1830]: 8. Structural variations and function diversity
                - paragraph [ref=e1831]: Protein-derived cofactors are remarkable products of posttranslational modifications that transform mundane amino acid residues into unique catalytic or structural moieties. These cofactors introduce novel functions such as redox switching, catalytic rate/efficiency amplification, adding a new function or converting a precursor protein to a functionally active enzyme, vastly expanding the functional repertoire of proteins. Even cofactors derived from identical amino acids can display distinct functions based on their position and conformations within their host enzymes. The evolution of these cofactors reflects nature's ability to optimize proteins for increasingly complex biological roles.
                - paragraph [ref=e1832]:
                  - text: A striking example of structural variation in a protein-derived cofactor is the two unique cofactor forms in KatG, MYW and MYW-OOH (
                  - link "Fig. 20" [ref=e1833] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig20"
                  - text: ). Previous crystallography studies identified an indole-
                  - emphasis [ref=e1834]: "N"
                  - text: "-linked hydroperoxyl group MYW-OOH cofactor, and recently this MYW-OOH form was extensively studied in solution and compared to the active MYW form."
                  - superscript [ref=e1835]:
                    - link "254" [ref=e1836] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit254"
                  - text: The MYW-OOH cofactor was first identified in crystal structures of KatG expressed at 25 °C, while the MYW form predominates in KatG expressed at 37 °C. Resonance Raman (rR), electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR), mass spectrometry, and X-ray crystallographic studies confirmed the reversible interconversion between these forms.
                - figure [ref=e1837]:
                  - heading "Fig. 20. Heme pocket of M. tuberculosis KatG with focus on MYW crosslink and His108, a catalytically relevant distal residue. (A) MYW form of KatG's cofactor that supports catalase activity (cyan; PDB 8W1X) shows a putative O2 molecule, presumably a decomposed product of the hydroperoxyl moiety. (B) Natural MYW-OOH form of KatG's cofactor that presents limited catalase activity (cyan; PDB 8W1W).254." [level=3] [ref=e1838]:
                    - text: Fig. 20. Heme pocket of
                    - emphasis [ref=e1839]: M. tuberculosis
                    - text: KatG with focus on MYW crosslink and His108, a catalytically relevant distal residue. (A) MYW form of KatG's cofactor that supports catalase activity (cyan; PDB 8W1X) shows a putative O
                    - subscript [ref=e1840]: "2"
                    - text: molecule, presumably a decomposed product of the hydroperoxyl moiety. (B) Natural MYW-OOH form of KatG's cofactor that presents limited catalase activity (cyan; PDB 8W1W).
                    - superscript [ref=e1841]:
                      - link "254" [ref=e1842] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#cit254"
                    - text: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e1843]:
                    - link "Fig. 20 🔍" [ref=e1844] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=11951088_d4cs00981a-f20.jpg
                      - img "Fig. 20" [ref=e1845]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1847] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/fig20/
                - paragraph [ref=e1848]:
                  - text: Structural variation is also evident in the Lys–Cys–NOS cofactor of
                  - emphasis [ref=e1849]: Neisseria gonorrhoeae
                  - text: transaldolase (
                  - emphasis [ref=e1850]: Ng
                  - text: TAL), which functions as a reversible redox switch. In its oxidized state, the cofactor contains a sulfur–oxygen–nitrogen–oxygen–sulfur (NOS) bridge, detectable
                  - emphasis [ref=e1851]: via
                  - text: sulfur K-edge X-ray absorption spectroscopy (XAS).
                  - superscript [ref=e1852]:
                    - link "289" [ref=e1853] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#cit289"
                  - text: Upon reduction, the NOS bridge is absent, and
                  - emphasis [ref=e1854]: Ng
                  - text: TAL becomes catalytically active, reverting to inactivity over time as the oxidized form reforms. Unlike most protein-derived cofactors, which enhance catalytic efficiency, the NOS bridge decreases
                  - emphasis [ref=e1855]: Ng
                  - text: TAL's catalytic activity. Computational studies indicate that the high-energy nature of the NOS bridge supports its reversibility, allowing the enzyme to cycle between active and inactive states. These features make the NOS cofactor an intriguing target for studies in protein engineering and drug discovery, particularly for applications requiring redox-controlled enzymatic switches.
                - paragraph [ref=e1856]: The structural plasticity of protein-derived cofactors highlights their adaptability in response to environmental or cellular cues. Variations in cofactor structure often directly influence enzymatic activity, substrate specificity, or regulatory mechanisms, underscoring the evolutionary ingenuity of these modifications. As seen with the MYW and NOS cofactors, structural variants may serve as molecular switches, toggling between states to regulate biological functions dynamically.
              - generic [ref=e1857]:
                - heading "9. Summary and perspective" [level=2] [ref=e1858]: 9. Summary and perspective
                - paragraph [ref=e1859]: "This review has explored the fascinating world of amino acid modifications and crosslinking in proteins, emphasizing the structural and functional diversity of protein-derived cofactors and their transformative role in enzymology. Originating from PTMs, these cofactors reveal a hidden layer of protein complexity, enabling enzymes to perform functions that extend beyond the capabilities of unmodified amino acid residues. In particular, the amino acid residue crosslinking process isn't just about molecular intricacies—it's about the creation of novel redox centers that significantly impact enzyme functionality. This burgeoning field has unveiled a new level of protein complexity, as evidenced by the discovery of protein-derived cofactors. Since the foundational work of Okeley and van der Donk two decades ago, which summarized 17 distinct cofactors, the field has expanded significantly, now encompassing 38 unique types (42 when free radical forms are counted), categorized into three major groups: single amino acid-derived cofactors, two-residue crosslinked cofactors, and trimeric crosslinked cofactors."
                - paragraph [ref=e1860]: A recurring theme in cofactor biogenesis is the involvement of metal centers. Protein-bound metals such as iron and copper often facilitate the complex chemical transformations necessary for cofactor formation, acting as catalytic mediators in autocatalytic or enzymatically driven pathways. While not all cofactors require metal centers, most characterized cases display the integral role of metalloproteins in enzymatic catalysis and the generation of protein-derived cofactors.
                - paragraph [ref=e1861]: The discovery of these protein-derived cofactors underscores the versatility and ingenuity of nature's enzymatic toolbox. Beyond enhancing catalytic efficiency, protein-derived cofactors drive the evolution of novel biochemical pathways and regulatory mechanisms. Advances in structural biology, including X-ray crystallography, cryo-electron microscopy, and emerging biophysical techniques, have been instrumental in identifying and characterizing these cofactors, paving the way for future discoveries.
                - paragraph [ref=e1862]: Despite these advances, significant challenges remain. The lack of predictive methods for identifying PTMs and the limited understanding of cofactor formation pathways highlight the need for further research. Protein-derived cofactors remain the most challenging aspect of protein structure–function relationships, for even the most advanced AI models. Research advances in this area will help generative AI grow stronger and more accurate in predicting protein structure and functions. Further studies will connect and integrate bioinformatics, structural biology, and metalloenzyme chemistry to elucidate the rules governing cofactor formation and function.
                - paragraph [ref=e1863]: It is important to note that twenty-two genetically coded amino acids aren’t enough for catalysis. At least 38 protein-derived cofactors play critical roles in enhancing or even introducing new catalytic activities in enzymes. As the field progresses, the study of protein-derived cofactors promises to yield new insights into enzymology, protein evolution, novel catalytic functions, and bioengineering. These insights will deepen our understanding of fundamental biological processes, and offer innovative strategies for applications in biotechnology, drug discovery, and synthetic biology.
              - generic [ref=e1864]:
                - heading "Data availability" [level=2] [ref=e1865]: Data availability
                - paragraph [ref=e1866]: All data supporting this study are included in the manuscript. All figures are original, created by the authors, and appropriately cited where external sources were used to support their illustrations.
              - generic [ref=e1867]:
                - heading "Conflicts of interest" [level=2] [ref=e1868]: Conflicts of interest
                - paragraph [ref=e1869]: There are no conflicts to declare.
              - generic [ref=e1870]:
                - heading "Acknowledgments" [level=2] [ref=e1871]: Acknowledgments
                - paragraph [ref=e1872]: This work was supported by the National Institutes of Health (NIH) under award numbers GM108988 and GM152982, the National Science Foundation (NSF) award under CHE-2204225, and the Welch Foundation grant AX-2110-20220331. A. L. acknowledges the generous support of the Lutcher Brown Endowment Fund.
              - generic [ref=e1873]:
                - heading "Biographies" [level=2] [ref=e1874]: Biographies
                - generic [ref=e1875]:
                  - heading "Biography" [level=3] [ref=e1876]
                  - figure [ref=e1877]:
                    - heading "Angelica Graciano ." [level=4] [ref=e1878]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1879]:
                      - img "Angelica Graciano" [ref=e1880]
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1882] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/d67e38_fig39/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1883]: Angelica Graciano is currently a Chemistry PhD student at the University of Texas at San Antonio (UTSA) under the supervision of Dr Aimin Liu. She obtained her BSc in Biochemistry at UTSA where her undergraduate research helped develop a deep interest in metalloenzymology. Her current research focuses on the elucidation of protein-derived cofactor biogenesis mechanisms through the incorporation of non-canonical amino acids, and characterization of metalloenzymes with functional roles in oxygen activation, amino acid metabolism, and carbohydrate metabolism.
                - generic [ref=e1884]:
                  - heading "Biography" [level=3] [ref=e1885]
                  - figure [ref=e1886]:
                    - heading "Aimin Liu ." [level=4] [ref=e1887]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1888]:
                      - img "Aimin Liu" [ref=e1889]
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1891] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/d67e58_fig39/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1892]: Dr Aimin Liu specializes in amino acid chemistry, including metabolism and crosslinking. His research explores redox chemistries mediated by metalloproteins and free radicals, with conceptual contributions like biological charge resonance. He earned his PhD from Stockholm University after a BS from USTC. He held Royal Society scholarships and began his independent career in 2002 and is currently an endowed distinguished Professor of Chemistry and the Lutcher Brown Distinguished Chair in Biochemistry, and a member of the Academy of Distinguished Researchers at the University of Texas at San Antonio. He is an AAAS Fellow (Chemistry) and FRSC.
